===== PAGE 1 =====
Gauge Theory and Skein Modules
Du Pei
Centre for Quantum Mathematics, University of Southern Denmark,
Campusvej 55, 5230 Odense, Denmark
dpei@imada.sdu.dk
Abstract
We study skein modules of 3-manifolds by embedding them into the Hilbert spaces
of 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theories. When the 3-manifold has reduced holonomy,
we present an algorithm to determine the dimension and the list of generators of the
skein module with a general gauge group. The analysis uses a deformation preserving
N= 1 supersymmetry to express the dimension as a sum over nilpotent orbits in its Lie
algebra. We find that the dimensions often differ between Langlands-dual pairs beyond
theA-series, for which we provide a physical explanation involving chiral symmetry
breaking and ’t Hooft operators. We also relate our results to the structure ofC ∗-fixed
loci in the moduli space of Higgs bundles. This approach helps to clarify the relation
between the gauge-theoretic framework of Kapustin and Witten with other versions
of the geometric Langlands program, explains why the dimensions of skein modules
do not exhibit a TQFT-like behavior, and provides a physical interpretation of the
skein-valued curve counting of Ekholm and Shende.
arXiv:2601.16213v2  [hep-th]  18 Feb 2026

1 Introduction
In this paper, we investigate skein modules of 3-manifolds, using supersymmetric gauge the-
ories and their infrared dynamics as our primary tools. The analysis involves a surprisingly
rich collection of quantum field theories in two, three, and four dimensions.
We begin by introducing the cast of characters and the puzzles that we aim to solve.
1.1 Skein modules
Three-manifold skein modules were first introduced in the context of the skein relations for
the Kauffman bracket polynomial [1, 2]. Given a connected and oriented three-manifold
M3, the Kauffman-bracket skein module sk(M 3) can be defined as theZ[A,A −1]-module
generated by links inM 3, modulo ambient isotopies and the skein relations,
*
 +
=A·
*
 +
+A−1·
*
 +
,(1.1)

L∪

= (−A2−A−2)·⟨L⟩.(1.2)
This construction has a generalization for an arbitrary braided ribbon categoryC[2], which
recovers the Kauffman-bracket skein module whenCis the Temperley–Lieb category. What
concerns us in this paper is the case ofC= Rep q(G), whereGis a connected reductive
algebraic group overC, and we study the corresponding skein module sk(M 3;G). This is a
family that directly generalizes the original skein modules, recovering sk(M3) via a change of
variable whenG= SL(2). Although our approach might also lead to interesting predictions
about the torsion part of the module, the main focus of this paper is on the case with the
value ofq∈C ∗ being generic. Equivalently, one can regard sk(M 3;G) as a vector space over
the field of fractionsQ(q).
The skein modules sk(M3;G) naturally arise in a variety of seemingly different settings in
mathematics (see [3] for a review). Several of these contexts are closely connected to physics,
including:
3

===== PAGE 5 =====
•The skein module can be viewed as the quantization of the character varietyM(M3;G)≃
Hom (π1(M3),G)/G, and reduces to its coordinate ring whenq= 1 [4–6].
•In quantum topology, sk(M 3;G) is the natural home for the quantum invariants of
links inM 3, in the sense that, whenM 3 is no longerS 3, the generalization for the
Jones polynomial or the Kauffman bracket is no longer a polynomial but instead an
element of sk(M3;G).
•Skein modules also have a natural place in the geometric Langlands correspondence [7].
In particular, they are naturally associated with three-manifolds in (an extension of)
the Betti version of the correspondence (see e.g. [8–10]).
While these three facts conceptually connect skein modules to quantum field theories
(QFTs) in, respectively, two (via the A-model with certain boundary conditions, see e.g. [11]
and references therein), three (via Chern–Simons theory [12]), and four dimensions, their
practical use in the study of skein modules has remained largely mysterious. One of our
missions is to clarify the connection between skein modules and physics so that we can
then use quantum field theories to make mathematical predictions about the structure and
properties of skein modules.
1.2 Puzzles
We start with the interplay with 4d theories, partially because results about QFTs in higher
dimensions can often subsume those in lower-dimensional ones. In fact, the last point in the
previous list, when combined with the gauge-theoretic approach to the geometric Langlands
program initiated in [13,14], seems to lead to the tempting identification,
sk(M3;G)
?
≃H(M 3;G),(1.3)
whereH(M 3;G) denotes the space of states (a.k.a. the Hilbert space) of the topologically
twisted 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory.
The two sides of (1.3) are actually comparable in the sense that both depend on the
choice of the 3-manifold and the gauge group, as well as the value of a complex parameter. 1
However, these two vector spaces are almost polar opposites, with the skein module being
1On the gauge theory side, the corresponding complex parameter is essentially the coupling constant
together with the theta angle. We also note that we label the Hilbert space of the gauge theory by the
algebraic groupGto conform with the notation for skein modules. The actual gauge group is the unique
(up to conjugacy) maximal compact subgroupKofG≃K C viewed as a complex Lie group. The difference
between the algebraic and analytic points of view is largely irrelevant for the results of this paper, and we
often do not carefully distinguish between them.
4

===== PAGE 6 =====
always finite dimensional due to a powerful theorem of [15], while the Hilbert spaceH(M3;G)
is almost always infinite dimensional.
Indeed, even for the simplest choice ofM=S 3, for which dim sk(M 3;G) = 1 for any
G, the Hilbert spaceH(M 3;G) contains a tower of states given byH ∗(BG) generated by
gauge-invariant combinations of the adjoint scalar in the theory. See [16] for a more detailed
analysis of the Hilbert space, where it was conjectured to be infinite dimensional for anyM 3.
After accepting the fact that (1.3) cannot be an isomorphism between vector spaces, one
might hope that the dimension on the right-hand side can be regularized—as is often needed
to define the partition function of a quantum field theory—leading to an equality
dim sk(M3;G)
?
= dim′H(M3;G).(1.4)
One natural way to regularize is to, instead of the dimension, compute the Witten index—
equivalently the graded dimension—in a massive phase of the theory. This has several issues,
one is that there might not be such a deformation to a massive theory that is compatible
with the topological twist; another is that, when such deformations exist, there might be no
canonical one.2 Lastly, even when deformations exist and they all give the same answer for
the Witten index, it can in general be different from the dimension of the skein module.
Below, we compare the two quantities forM 3 =T 3:
G SL(2) SL(3) SL(4) SL(5) GL(5) G2 F4
dim sk(T 3;G) 9 29 75 131 7 24 123
Witten index 10 30 84 130 0 30 227
. . .
Here, the results for SL and GL groups are taken from [17], whereas the results for the
exceptional groups are our predictions, which will be explained later together with the com-
putation of the Witten indices.
In this table, one finds that the two rows are almost never identical. For the SL-series,
one might have guessed from the first two data points that the answers always differ by one,
and this discrepancy for SL(2) was noted in [11] and is sometimes known as the “10-vs-9”
paradox. But this pattern does not persist either within or beyond the SL-series. The Witten
index can be either larger (e.g. SL(4)) or smaller (e.g. SL(5)), and they can be within 15%
“error margin” (e.g. SL(N) for smallN), have a larger gap (e.g. the exceptional series or
classical groups with large rank), or be qualitatively different (e.g. GL(N)).
2Different massive phases of the gauge theory can have different Witten indices, which are only defor-
mation invariant if the theory remains massive but can jump as we cross walls where massless degrees of
freedom emerge.
5

===== PAGE 7 =====
ForM 3 = Σ×S 1, with Σ a genus-gRiemann surface, the mismatch becomes worse. On
the side of the skein module, one has [18,19]
dim sk(Σ×S 1; SL(2)) = 22g+1 + 2g−1,(1.5)
while the Witten index is given by 3
I(Σ×S 1) = 22g+1 + 2.(1.6)
From the field-theory point of view, what is more concerning is not the fact that the difference
between the two formulae grows with the genus, but rather the non-TQFT behavior of (1.5),
due to the presence of the term that is linear ing. In other words, while (1.6) is of the form
expected from the partition function of a TQFT, the formula in (1.5) is not, and one should
not expect to obtain it from a TQFT in a simple fashion.
This touches upon a bigger issue—the non-TQFT behavior in (the Betti version of) the
geometric Langlands program, which is itself puzzling if one views the correspondence as
originating from theS-duality of the topologically twisted 4dN= 4 theory, which is, by
construction, topological.
1.3 The solution
We will resolve these puzzles, starting with a more precise understanding of the relation
between the skein module and the Hilbert space of the 4d gauge theory, which will ultimately
allow us to generalize (1.5) to other groups. Schematically, the dimension formula for a simply
connectedGtakes the following form,
dim sk(Σ×S 1;G)∼
X
λ
|Cλ|2g+1·Γ (Gλ,cλ·(2g−2)),(1.7)
which is a sum over conjugacy classesλofsl 2-triples ing(or, equivalently, nilpotent orbits
Oλ⊂g), with the summand being a product of two factors. The first factor counts the num-
ber of flatC λ-connections over Σ×S 1 withC λ :=π 0(Gλ) denoting the group of components
of the centralizer subgroupG λ⊂Gof the triple. The second factor Γ (G λ,cλ·(2g−2))
counts the number of dominant weights ofG λ at levelc λ·(2g−2)—withc λ related to the
embedding index ofG λ⊂G—modulo the action of the center symmetryZ(G).
To obtain the actual dimension formula and to incorporate cases with non-simply con-
nectedG, there are a few modifications to (1.7) that will be explained later. We will
3This is with a particular deformation which we will explain later. For another deformation used in [16],
the discrepancy is even larger.
6

===== PAGE 8 =====
also study its doubly graded refinement by electric and magnetic fluxes valued ine∈
H1(M3,Z(G)∨) andm∈H 2(M3,π 1(G)) coming from the electric and magnetic 1-form
symmetries of the 4d gauge theory. For now, we will give some examples forG= SL(N) or
GL(N), for which no modification of (1.7) is needed.
1.3.1 Examples
When the genusg= 1, we have Γ(G λ,0) = 1. ForG= SL(N) or GL(N), the nilpotent
orbits are labeled by partitions ofN. The formula (1.7) becomes
dim sk(Σ×S 1;G) =
X
λ⊢N
|Cλ|3.(1.8)
While the groupC λ is trivial for GL(N), forG= SL(N) it is given byC λ =Z gcd(λ), a cyclic
group of order equal to the greatest common divisor of all parts of the partition, and thus
(1.7) recovers the results in [17].
On the other hand, for generalg, the formula reproduces (1.5) whenG= SL(2), but
yields new predictions for other Lie groups. For example, whenG= SL(3) or PSL(3), the
formula gives
dim sk(Σ×S 1; SL(3)) = 32g+1 + 3(2g+ 1)(g−1) + 1 +δ g,1.(1.9)
The appearance of the Kronecker deltaδ g,1 turns out to be a general feature of contributions
from “intermediate”sl 2-triples with a non-semisimple centralizerG λ.
1.3.2 Strategy
In deriving (1.7), we utilize the following three main ideas.
Embedding into gauge theory.We propose an embedding of sk(M 3;G) into the dual
ofH(M 3;G),
sk(M3;G)⊂H(M 3;G)∨,(1.10)
which we believe clarifies the relation between them. Alternatively, this realizes the dual of
the skein module as a quotient,
sk(M3;G)∨≃H(M 3;G)/∼.(1.11)
This embedding/quotient utilizes a setup similar to those in [20–23] used to study quantum
invariants of knots and 3-manifolds and their categorifications. This proposal allows us
to readily recover several known results on sk(M 3) in cases whereH(M 3) has a simple
description, such as whenM 3≃S 3/Γ is spherical.
7

===== PAGE 9 =====
AnN= 1deformation.When anM 3 has reduced holonomy, a powerful computational
tool for the dimension of sk(M 3;G) is theN= 1 deformation studied in [24,25]. Combined
with the proposed embedding (1.10), this gives the dimension of sk(T3) for anyGvia counting
vacua in the deformed theory modulo certain equivalence relations, which explains the 10-
vs-9 paradox. Furthermore, it leads to a surprising finding that the dimensions are often
different for Langlands dual pairs,
dim sk(T3;G)̸= dim sk(T 3;LG) (1.12)
once we go beyond theA-series. WhenM 3 = Σ×S 1, the deformation will give rise to
“cosmic strings”—surface defects with massless degrees of freedom on their worldsheet, and
one technical challenge to overcome is to understand how they contribute to sk(M 3). This
leads to the next point.
Bulk–string coupling.It turns out that we will need to understand the action of the
bulk Wilson lines on the Hilbert space of the cosmic string, which we argue can be modeled
by the coupled system of a Chern–Simons bulk and a WZW/free-fermion boundary theory
after compactifying on the “meridian circle.” This allows us to obtain the dimension formula
(1.7) and to give an algorithm to produce the set of generators.
Organization of the paper
The paper is organized around the sequential introduction and exploration of the three ideas
outlined above. In Section 2, we discuss the relation between sk(M 3) and the Hilbert space
of the 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory. In Section 3, we use theN= 1 deformation
to study skein modules onT 3. In Section 4, we incorporate cosmic strings to complete
the computation for sk(Σ×S 1). We then examine the extent to which our results are
compatible with Langlands duality in Section 5, where we also study the connection with
the geometry of the moduli space of Higgs bundles. In Section 6, we briefly explore some
other applications of our approach—emphasizing new perspectives on existing results—and
outline some interesting future directions.
2 Embedding
We first outline the construction for embedding the 3-manifold skein module into the Hilbert
space of the 4dN= 4 theory.
8

===== PAGE 10 =====
2.1 Summary of the construction
To study theG-skein module sk(M 3;G,q) for the connected reductive algebraic groupG
via gauge theory, it is convenient to viewGinstead as a complex Lie group. Its maximal
compact Lie subgroup isK, which will be the gauge group of our UV theory. A well-known
result on the structure ofGandKstates thatK= (U(1) r× eH)/Fis a finite quotient of a
product of an abelian part with a simply-connected semisimple compact Lie group eH. The
“non-abelian part” ofKis given by the commutator subgroupH:= [K,K] and is a quotient
of eHby a subgroup of its centerZ( eH).
We then consider the topologically twisted 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory onM 3×R+
with gauge groupK. The boundary condition at the originx= 0 ofR + is the one used in [20]
for discussions of the analytically continued Chern–Simons theory and the categorification
of the Jone polynomial, which is the “tip of the cigar” boundary condition when lifted to
higher dimensions (see also [22, 23] where a similar setup is used in the study of invariants
of 3-manifolds), and it is expected that insertions of Wilson lines alongM 3 atx= 0 are
equivalent up to ambient isotopy and skein relations [20,21].
ViewingR + as the time direction andx= 0 as either the beginning or the end of time
gives a state|L⟩in the Hilbert spaceH(M 3) of the 4d theory onM 3 or⟨L|in its dual,
whereLencodes information about the insertion of Wilson lines. We will take the latter
perspective,4 viewing the collectionLof line operators as a functional onH(M 3) and define
S(M3;G,q) = span{⟨L|}⊂H(M 3)∨ (2.1)
as the space of functionals spanned by all such insertions. Here,q:=e 2πiΨ with Ψ being the
“canonical parameter” formed out of the coupling constantτof the gauge theory and a twist
parametert(see Appendix A for more details). This construction is illustrated in Figure 1.
In one direction, it is obvious that
sk(M3;G,q)→S(M 3;G,q) (2.2)
is surjective by construction. How to show that the map is also injective? Injectivity equiv-
alently means that any pairLandL ′ in sk(M 3;G,q) that are not equivalent can always
be distinguished by a state|ψ⟩with⟨L|ψ⟩̸=⟨L ′|ψ⟩. As sk(M 3) is the quantization of the
character variety and the wave function ofLandL ′ can be probed by⟨L|α⟩and⟨L ′|α⟩
withαbeing a flat connection. Intuitively, if they agree for allα, then they have the same
quantum wave function and should also agree as elements in the skein module.
4One reason is that we typically prefer to talk about normalizable states, whereas|L⟩is expected to be
not normalizable. On the other hand, the dualH(M 3)∨ is bigger and can actually contain⟨L|.
9

===== PAGE 11 =====
Figure 1: A boundary condition of the topologically twisted 4d theory onM 3×R + with
insertions of Wilson lines alongL⊂M 3 is illustrated on the upper left. Upon compactifi-
cation onM 3, this gives a boundary conditionB L for the quantum mechanics onR + (lower
part of the figure), which gives a state⟨L|in the dual of its Hilbert spaceH(M 3)∨ (upper
right).
Based on this reasoning—as well as the “naturalness” of the construction (2.1)—we
conjecture that
sk(M3;G,q)≃S(M 3;G,q) (2.3)
are isomorphic as vector spaces. This enables us to access the skein modules of three-
manifolds using QFT techniques, which, in turn, allows us to test this conjecture by com-
paring its predictions with known results.
2.2 ’t Hooft operators and magnetic sectors
One might be concerned that the construction of the subspace/quotientS(M 3) is not com-
patible with theS-duality of the 4dN= 4 theory, which exchange Wilson and ’t Hooft line
operators [26, 27]. Therefore, the identification ofS(M 3) with the skein module ofM 3 does
not automatically guarantee the duality between sk(M 3,G) and sk(M 3,LG).
Therefore, it might be tempting to define a duality-invariant versioneSby allowing also ’t
Hooft operators in the construction (2.1), which live on a D5-like boundary condition. Due
to Witten effect, they will become dyonic underT-transformations, and, to have the full
SL(2,Z) symmetry, one should include all dyonic lines on all (p,q)-type boundary conditions.
10

===== PAGE 12 =====
We will denote the resulting vector space as eS. Then, automatically, one has
eS(M3;G,q)≃ eS(M3;LG,Lq),(2.4)
where Lq=e
−2πi 1
ngΨ , withn g being the lacing number ofg. 5 From the point of view of
duality-invariance, it is certainly advisable to work with eSinstead, but its connection with
the skein module is less straightforward. Indeed, the dual boundary conditions are not
very “Chern–Simons–like” and it is not clear how to incorporate the line operators on these
boundary conditions in a simple fashion into skein theory. Despite of this, we will refer to eS
as the “enriched skein module.”
One might hope that going fromSto eSwould not actually enlargeSin a meaningful
way. One justification would be that Wilson and ’t Hooft operators behave as canonical dual
pairs (e.g. like position and momentum), and one could generate the entire representation,
on which both of them act, only using one set of the operators.
However, there is one important subtlety, as the above is only true if one starts with a
“generic state.” On the other hand, if the state one starts with is not generic enough, one
would not be able to generate the entire representation only using Wilson lines, but instead
get only a subspace. This clearly happens whenπ 1(K) =π 1(G) has torsion and there are
different topological types ofG-bundles labeled by the magnetic fluxm∈H 2(M3,π 1(G)).
Indeed, in such a situation, an ’t Hooft line can change the topological type of the gauge
bundle which can never be achieved by a Wilson line insertion.
Partly motivated by this, we consider a slight generalization ofSwhenGis not simply
connected by also taking into account the existence of different “magnetic sectors,”
S(M3;G) =
M
m
Sm(M3;G),(2.5)
where eachS m(M3;G) is generated by Wilson lines alone in the presence of the given
magnetic-flux background.6 Adding back the magnetic sectors “by hand” allows us to only
work with Wilson lines, as well as providing a conceptual explanation why the Langlands
duality for skein modules can (sometimes) hold even though the skein modules do not seem
to incorporate the ’t Hooft lines.
5WhenGis not simple, one can make the coupling constant for each abelian or simple summand ofg
independent. Therefore,qis in fact a collection of complex numbers, withn g in the formula also understood
as a collection of integers. As our focus is on the case withqgeneric, we will not keep track of this collection
and will simply denote it asqor suppress it altogether.

also encounter cases (e.g. dualB–Cpairs) where the duality forSfails, but can be restored
by passing to the enriched version eS.
2.3 Electric sectors
The Hilbert spaceH(or its dual) also admits a grading by electric fluxese∈H 1(M3,Z(G)∨)

eandm. (See also [28, 13, 29, 30] for related discussions.) For a state inSrepresented by
a configuration of Wilson lines, their charges underZ(G) determine aZ(G) ∨-valued 1-cycle
which in turn determinese.
A “dual” way of interpreting the electric flux is the following. The 1-form symmetry in
4d becomes anH 1(M3,Z(G)) 0-form symmetry acting on the Hilbert spaceH(M 3) of the
quantum mechanics after compactification. This gives an isotypical decomposition of the
Hilbert space into sectors labeled by characters,

Equivalently, one can use the perfect pairing betweenH1(M3,Z(G)) andH 1(M3,Z(G)∨) that

2.4 Examples
Without much work, this embedding of sk(M3) into gauge theory already allows us to recover
some familiar yet non-trivial results about skein modules.
Connected sum.For a test of the construction, consider the connected sumM 3#M′
3 of
two 3-manifoldsM 3 andM′
3. Using topological invariance, one can stretch the two summands
far apart, connected via a long tubeS 2×[−l,l] withl≫1. One can then viewH(M 3#M′
3)
as the Hilbert space of theS 2-compactification of the 4d theory on the interval [−l,l] with
two boundary conditions. At low energy, theS 2-compactification is described by a 2d (4,4)
sigma model with target (k4)/Wparametrized by four of of the six adjoint scalars that remain
scalars onS 2. This can be constructed as a symmetric orbifold of a free theory, and the
UV Wilson lines are heavy and become defect operators. Therefore, Wilson lines stretched
between the two boundaries will cost energy and such states will not be in the IR Hilbert
space. Topological invariance then implies that they pair trivially with any incoming states.
As a consequence, the Wilson lines can only be inserted at the two boundaries, and we have
a factorization,
S(M3#M′
3) =S(M 3)⊗S(M ′
3),(2.10)
recovering a well-known property of skein modules.
If we identifyZ(g)≃coker(A g) as the co-kernel of the Cartan matrixA g, the pairing (2.8) is then given by
the inverse of the Cartan matrixA −1
g . Whengis not semisimple andZ(g) is not discrete, it turns out that
one only needs the component groupπ 0Z(g) as only the torsion part ofZ(g)∨ is expected to give a non-trivial
Hilbert space in the topologically twisted theory. This is dual to the statement that the meaningful labels
for magnetic fluxes live in the torsion subgroup ofπ 1(G).
13

===== PAGE 15 =====
Another case where our construction can be readily applied to compute the skein module
is when the character varietyM(M 3;G) is a collection of points. In these cases, one can
argue that the gauge theory onM 3 at low energy decomposes into vacua labeled by the flat
connections. In each vacuum, the expectation values of all Wilson lines are determined by
the flat connection, and their spanS(M 3;G) is the same as the span of the flat connections
or, equivalently,C[M(M 3;G)].
Lens spaces.To give a concrete example, consider a lens spaceM 3 =L(p,r) andG=
SL(2). The moduli space consists of⌊ p

as a multi-wrapped Wilson line can always be decomposed. The sectors of the 4d theory
onL(p,r) are labeled by SL(2) flat connections diag{α j,α−1

of states above each vacuum as they cannot be distinguished from each other using Wilson
lines.8
While these infinite towers do not cause much problem in examples with isolatedM(M3),
they appear to be more troublesome to deal with whenM(M 3) has higher-dimensional
components, where one can no longer count the number of flat connections but instead has
to quantize the classical configurations to find the quantum vacua. The extreme case of this,
in the sense of being the polar opposite of having an isolatedM, is whenM 3 = Σ×S 1.
Understanding skein modules for such manifolds is one of the main goals of this paper,
and the solution neatly showcases the power of the QFT approach. One essential ingredient

in our construction, which we will leave for future work. Another interesting question is whether there are
more states beyond these towers. Whenp>1, there are hints of additional states from a higher-dimensional
perspective by considering the 6d (2,0) theory onL(p,1)×Σ×R, which gives rise to a quantum mechanics
whose index is an infinite power series for a general Riemann surface Σ [31]. When Σ =T 2, there is a
massive deformation that yields a finite-dimensional quantum mechanics, which is theS 1-compactification
of a 2d theory with massive vacua in bijection with the flat connections onL(p,1) [32, Sec. 3.1].

is a deformation of the 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory toN= 1, which we will discuss
next. Readers interested in learning more about the details of the construction are directed
to Appendix A.
3 Deformation
The idea of using a deformation preservingN= 1 supersymmetry to study the topologically
twisted theory originates from [24] while what is more relevant for the present work is the
application of this technique to the Vafa–Witten theory [25], asH(M 3) in our setup can be
also identified with that of the Vafa–Witten theory. One difference is that our focus is more
on the Hilbert space as opposed to the partition function.
Recall that theN= 4 theory, when viewed as anN= 1 theory, has three adjoint chiral
multipletsT,UandVwith the superpotential,
W= TrT[U,V],(3.1)
and we consider theN= 1 deformation given by adding another term, 9
W′ =− m

Turning on such an interaction will cause the theory to decompose into a collection of sectors
labeled by critical points ofW+W ′, where the fields are constant and satisfy
[U,V] =mT,(3.3)
[V,T] =mU,(3.4)
[T,U] =mV.(3.5)
Modulo gauge transformations, the solutions are labeled by conjugacy classesλofsl 2-triples

3.1 Phases and nilpotent orbits
In principle, one can avoid talking about nilpotent orbits and just study the vacua via the
sl2-triple. However, it turns out to be quite useful and illuminating to make the connection,
bringing the field-theory analysis closer to the standard toolkit of (geometric) representation
theorists. Even for physicists, this provides a cleaner way to organize the IR phases of the
9This is sometimes known as theN= 1 ∗ deformation. In fact, we need a slightly different deformation
when considering the theory on a more general K¨ ahler manifold, which we will review later.
15

===== PAGE 17 =====
theory, especially whenGis not a classical Lie group. See [33, 34] for nice introductions of
the subject of nilpotent orbits and also [35,36] for closely related appearances of them in the
N= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory.
For a general complex reductiveG, we label the nilpotent orbitsO λ also byλ, which in
turn labels a phase of the deformed theory. The gauge groupKwill be broken by the vevs
of the adjoint scalars to the centralizer subgroupK λ of thesl 2-triple.
In general, this group, as well as its complexificationG λ, has more than one component.
We have the short exact sequence,
G0
λ→G λ→C λ,(3.6)
withG 0
λ the identity component ofG λ andC λ :=G λ/G0
λ the group of components.
A well-known result is that, whenGis semisimple and simply-connected,C λ is isomorphic
to the fundamental group of the nilpotent orbitπ 1(Oλ). For more generalG, it is a quotient
ofπ 1(Oλ) and can be found in tables (e.g. [33, Ch. 8.4]). On the opposite end, whenGis
of adjoint type,C λ≃A(O λ) which is known as the “component group.” Whengis either
sl(N) orsp(N),C λ is always abelian, which is an important simplification, as its number of
flat connections on anyM 3 is simple to count and given by|H 1(M3,Cλ)|.10
InsideK 0

N= 1 theory with the non-abelian gauge groupH λ will have confinement in the infrared, and
the classical vacuum for theH λ gauge theory will split into a collection of massive quantum
vacua. (See [37] for an excellent review on the dynamics of 4dN= 1 gauge theories.) Aside
from this degeneracy, at long distances, one only sees the gauge groupK IR
λ =K λ/Hλ. It is
given by the semi-direct productC λ ⋉K 0

continuous parts of the IR gauge group. The structure ofG 0
λ can be described uniformly
for classical groups [38] and can be found in [39,40] for exceptional groups. See also [38,41]
where much detail on nilpotent orbits and their centralizers is presented.
The phase diagram of the theory is parametrized by thesl 2-triple and, modulo gauge
transformations, becomes the Hasse diagram for the partial order ofOλ given by orbit closure.
The largest orbit in the partial order is known as the principal/regular orbit, for whichK
is broken down to its centerZ(K), and the infrared dynamics is described—assumingK
is semisimple—by a finite-group gauge theory. On the opposite end is the zero orbit that
corresponds to the trivialsl 2-triple. In this phase,K λ =Kin the UV but the non-abelian
partH λ =H:= [K,K] becomes confined in the IR, leaving a free abelian theory with gauge

Here, “type” is referring to a classification of phases based on the fate of the center of the
semisimple partHof the gauge groupKin the IR. To make the classification independent
of the choice of the global form ofK(e.g. SU vs PSU), we consider the variant withHbeing
simply connected. There are in general four possibilities.
Electric phase (E).This is when (a part of)Z(H) survives at low energy, becoming
eitherC λ by itself or a non-trivial subgroup ofC λ and part of the discrete gauge symmetry
in the IR. WhenKis semisimple with a non-trivial center, the phase corresponding to the
principal orbit is always an electric phase, where the gauge symmetry is completely broken
down toZ(K) by the vevs of the scalars and one has aZ(K) gauge theory at long distances.
Electric phases cannot exist if eitherZ(H) is trivial or contained in the identity component
ofZ(K) whenKis not semisimple. One example of such isK= U(N), for which the center
ofH= SU(N) is insideZ(K) = U(1) and becomes part of a U(1) gauge theory in the
infrared in every phase.
Magnetic phase (M).This is when (a part of)Z(H) lives inH λ and disappears in the
IR due to confinement. We additionally require that the IR phase is gapped. 12 The zero

orbit always corresponds to such a phase whenKis semisimple with a non-trivial center, as
the entireKsurvives the deformation and becomes confined in the IR. One non-example is
againK= U(N), for which the center ofH= SU(N) can be confined, but the IR phase is
never gapped.
Neutral phase (N).This is whenZ(H) doesn’t appear in eitherC λ orZ(H λ). In other
words, it is part of the identity component of the IR gauge groupK IR,0
λ . When this happens,
the Hilbert space of the IR theory doesn’t have non-trivial electric- or magnetic-flux sectors—
hence the term “neutral.” This can be the only type of phases whenZ(H) = 0 (e.g.K=
G2,F 4,E 8) or whenZ(H) is contained in the identity component ofZ(K) (e.g.K= U(N)).
A more interesting neutral phase (i.e. whenKis not of the kind mentioned above) is the
λ= [21] phase withK= SU(3), where the unbroken gauge symmetry is U(1) and the center
Z3 ofKis fully contained in it.
Hybrid phase (H).This is the opposite of the neutral phase: in a hybrid phase, one can
turn on either electric or magnetic background. There are actually two subtypes: HO and
HE. The former stands for “ordinary hybrid” phase. In such a phase, a subgroup ofZ(H) is
inZ(H λ) and disappears in the IR due to confinement, while a quotient is inCλ and becomes
part of the discrete gauge symmetry in the IR. Therefore, strictly speaking, it is also both
an electric and a magnetic phase (although we will only count them as HO phases in order
to make the labels as mutually exclusive as possible). One example for an HO phase is the
one labeled by the partitionλ= [22] forK= SU(4). AZ 2 subgroup of theZ 4 center will
become the center of the unbroken SU(2) that confines, while theZ 2 quotient will be the
gauge group in the gapped IR phase. On the other hand, an exceptional hybrid (HE) phase
is one that looks naively like an E/M/N/HO phase with (a part of)Z(H) insideK IR,0
λ but
has non-trivial flux sectors becauseK IR
λ is non-abelian and built out ofC λ andK IR,0
λ via
a non-trivial extension. Because of this, the electric symmetry could still act non-trivially
onπ 0M(M3,K IR
λ ) and one might still be able to turn on a magnetic flux. We will study
this phenomenon in more detail later, but we remark now that being HE is in principle not
mutually exclusive with the other labels. In other words, one can have phases of subtypes
E-HE, M-HE, N-HE, and HO-HE. However, in the examples we will study, the HE phases
encountered are often of the type N-HE, for which we will omit the N part in the notation.
in “boring” cases such as whenK= U(1)×Hhas an abelian factor, or in highly contrived cases not relevant
for us. Either the gapped condition or this technically more accurate condition is meant to ensure that
we can turn on ’t Hooft fluxes forHat no cost of energy. When the extension is non-trivial, an ’t Hooft
flux requires an accompanying first Chern class for the abelian part, and such a sector will have a non-zero
ground-state energy.

The last two rows in the table list the contribution of each phase to the Witten indexI
and the dimension of theT 3 skein module. While it is clear that the index can be computed
as a sum over IR phases, for the dimension of the skein module, this is not quite clear (and
turns out to be affected by subtle phenomena such as “overlaps” between phases). We now
examine more carefully how the deformation interacts with our proposed embedding of the
skein module.
3.2 Skein modules from deformation
AnN= 1 theory cannot be topologically twisted on a general 4-manifold, but can be twisted
if the 4-manifold is K¨ ahler. In our setup, where the 4-manifold is taken to beM 3×Rwith
a closedM 3, a topological twist of the deformed theory is only possible whenM 3 = Σ×S 1,
which is what we will mostly focus on in the remainder of the paper.
In the Vafa–Witten theory, the partition function is expected to be invariant under the
deformation, since the deformation (moduloQ-exact terms) has a positive “ghost number.”
Also, all the “intermediate vacua” withK IR
λ having a continuous part will not contribute to
the partition function due to the presence of fermionic zero modes.
Our focus is more on the Hilbert space as opposed to the partition function, though the
M3×S 1 partition function on—equivalently the Witten index of the 1d theory—serves as
an important reference point. As a consequence, vacua with continuousK IR
λ docontribute
toS, and taking them into account correctly turns out to be crucial to reproducing known
results on skein modules.
A common supercharge
In order to apply this deformation to study skein modules, one needs to make sure that it is
compatible with our construction. Naively, two different topological twists and supercharges
are used in the process, withH(M 3) being the cohomology of a Kapustin–Witten supercharge
Qt, while the deformation is the one used in the Vafa–Witten theory. Although these two
twists are different on generalM 4, their R-symmetry backgrounds become identical when
M4 =M 3×R. Also, the superchargeQ t actually becomes a supercharge for the Vafa–Witten
theory whent→∞(see Appendix A).
From now on, we will work with this “shared” supercharge—which we simply denote by
Q—so that the deformation interacts nicely with the embedding of skein modules. One price
we pay for this choice is that, as Ψ =τfor this value ofthas a positive imaginary part,
q=e 2πiτ now lives in the interior of the unit disk, making it difficult to access roots of unity
19

===== PAGE 21 =====
and hence torsion phenomena in skein modules. 13
The strategy we will employ to study skein modules is to consider pairings between states
⟨L|∈Sand vacuum states in various IR phases. In addition to the standard assumptions
about the IR physics of gauge theories, we will require a few further assumptions to maintain
control overSalong the RG flow.
Assumptions on RG flows
Eachλlabels a phase of the theory with an IR Hilbert spaceH λ(M3). In nice cases, one
expects that L
λHλ(M3)⊂ H(M3) is a subspace, which is more likely to hold for the
topologically twisted theory compared to the full theory. One can ask whether this leads to
a homomorphism
S→
M
λ
Hλ(M3)∨.(3.7)
Physically, this map should be essentially about the expectation values of UV Wilson lines
over different IR vacua. To make sure that it is well defined, one needs to check that this
vev only depends on skein classes of the Wilson loops. This is expected to follow from the
fact that two states⟨L|and⟨L ′|only differ by aQ-exact vector ifLandL ′ are in the same
skein class. Then, modulo non-compactness issues (e.g. boundary terms associated with
total derivatives), their pairings with anyQ-closed state are identical.
One can in principle directly verify this invariance in each of the IR phases. When the
IR theory is gapped, either due to Higgsing or confinement, it is clear that the vev of Wilson
loops only depends on the skein class. In more complicated cases (e.g. HE phases), this is less
obvious and requires a more careful analysis. In the next section, we will incorporate “cosmic
string” defects, which may also support massless degrees of freedom on their worldsheet even
when the bulk is gapped. While it would be interesting to understand whether these massless
modes could lead to subtleties, we will not pursue this here. Instead we assume this map to
be well defined for allλ, even when there are massless photons and/or cosmic strings in the
IR.
We will in fact make a stronger assumption that the map (3.7) is still injective—in other
words, the skein module still embeds into the IR Hilbert space with no new equivalence
relations. The justification for this assumption is that the pairing,⟨L|ψ⟩, if it is non-zero to
begin with, is expected to remain unchanged by the deformation based on a similar ghost-
number argument. This is not yet a complete explanation with some details that remain to
13It would be interesting to understand whether there are similar “controllable” deformations for other
values oft, so thatqbeing a root of unity is no longer a strong-coupling limit. Alternatively, one can work
in theS-dual frame with the Nahm-pole boundary condition decorated with ’t Hooft loops, which is now
weakly coupled.
20

===== PAGE 22 =====
be checked.14 We hope to return to this technical point in the future, but our strategy in
this paper is to justify this assumption by demonstrating that it leads to sensible results.
Another assumption we will make to facilitate the computation is that the pairing⟨L|v,λ⟩
between⟨L|and a vacuum state|v,λ⟩in the phaseλis proportional to the vacuum expec-
tation value of the Wilson loop(s) in that vacuum,⟨L⟩ v,λ. One justification is that, in the
IR theory which is gapped except for a decoupled free abelian sector, the Neumann-like
boundary condition atx= 0 is expected to behave as a “mirror,” 15 and the state⟨∅|pairing
with any vacua gives a constant (which we can normalize to 1). Also, the vacuum states
are now eigenstates of any Wilson loops, with the eigenvalue being the expectation value.
Combining the two points naturally gives
⟨L|v,λ⟩=⟨∅|L|v,λ⟩=⟨L⟩ v,λ.(3.8)
Equivalently, we can express the state⟨L|∈H ∨ as
⟨L|=
X
v,λ
⟨L⟩v,λ·(⟨v,λ|+···).(3.9)
Here “···” stands for the non-vacuum states above⟨v,λ|, which will play interesting roles
forM 3 = Σ×S 1 later. They are also the reason why⟨L|is in general not normalizable and
lives in the dual of the Hilbert space, even after the deformation.
With these assumptions, it seems that we have a concrete procedure to determine the
skein module:
1. Use the mass deformation toN= 1 to obtain a collection of vacua|v,λ⟩.
2. Find the vacuum expectation values of Wilson loops for different vacua.
3. Identify Wilson loops with the same expectation values for any choice of|v,λ⟩.
There turns out to be an important subtlety when the genusgof Σ is not equal to 1, as the
deformation parameter is actually a section of a line bundle, which can have poles or zeroes.
When it must have a pole, it is not clear how to turn on the deformation continuously, as the
deformation parameter is always large in some area ofM3. This can potentially lead to jumps
14For example, one should argue that a sufficiently large collection of test states|ψ⟩survive the deformation
and the RG flow so that they can continue to discern different skein classes. Notice that this should be a
non-trivial consequence of the ghost-number conservation and other properties of the twisted theory, as in
general, one can “lose” states in the IR (e.g. due to confinement). As a starting point for this analysis, it
would be beneficial to better understand the behavior of the boundary condition and the Wilson lines in the
twisted theory under deformation.
15This is in line with the higher-dimensional perspective viewing it as the tip of a cigar.
21

===== PAGE 23 =====
ofS. When the deformation parameter is forced to have a zero, there is no problem with
continuity, but the opposite problem arises—there are regions inM 3 where the deformation
is small, and the RG argument can fail. We won’t offer a solution to the problem caused
by the pole, which only arises forg= 0 (i.e.M 3 =S 2×S 1), but will deal with the latter
problem in the next section. In the remainder of the present section, we will focus on the
g= 1 case, where this subtlety doesn’t arise and the above procedure already gives new
predictions for skein modules.
3.3 Examples forsk(T 3)
We are now well-equipped to perform the computation forM 3 =T 3. To be pedagogical,
we first analyze the cases ofG= SL(2), SL(3) and their PSL versions in great detail before
dealing with the general case.
3.3.1SL(2)
When the gauge group of the UV theory isK= SU(2), there is an electric phase labeled by
the non-trivial embedding (corresponding to the principal orbit)λ= [2] described by aZ 2
gauge theory in the infrared. It has eight different vacua onT 3 given by the 2 3 = 8 choices
of holonomies. In this infrared phase,H λ=[2](T 3)≃C 8. Any UV Wilson line labeled by
an irreducible representation of SU(2) becomes a multiple of either the trivial line 1 or the
non-trivialZ 2 Wilson lineφ. Any vacuum can be distinguished from the rest via vevs ofZ 2
Wilson loops, but one needs all eight configurations of Wilson loops obtained by inserting
them along some of the three cycles ofT 3. In theZ 2 phase, the fusion rule for Wilson lines is
φ2 = 1. It is clear that any other configurations are equivalent to one of the eight. Therefore,
the mapS→H λ=[2](T 3)∨≃C 8 is surjective.
For the trivialsl(2)-triple (zero orbit)λ= [1 2], the theory is in a magnetic phase. It
has an unbroken SU(2) gauge group, which is confined in the IR. There are two additional
quantum vacua with gaugino condensate⟨λλ⟩=±Λ 1/2. In the deep IR where all massive
degrees of freedom is integrated out, one hasHλ=[12](T 3)≃C 2 being a two-dimensional space
spanned by the two vacua,

Before concluding that the skein module is 8+1=9 dimensional, it remains to show that
any one of the confining vacua is distinguishable from the other 8 states in theZ2 phase. This
can be argued by noticing that, as these two vacua are confining, insertion of any non-trivial
Wilson line should give zero correlation function. To detect it, it is not enough to just use
the eight configurations of Wilson lines, as a linear combination of the eight states in theZ 2
vacuum of the form
|→⟩= 1
2

2(|+⟩+|−⟩) ⊗3,(3.10)
which is a superposition of all the 8 states, cannot be distinguished from one of the con-
fining vacua using only these configurations. Indeed, the vev for any of the 7 non-trivial
configurations will be zero on both this state and a confining vacuum. To distinguish them,
one needs to introduce another configuration, which can be taken to be an adjoint Wilson
loop along an arbitrary cycle ofT 3. This would be the same as the trivial configuration in
theZ 2 vacua, evaluating to 3—the dimension of the adjoint representation—on|→⟩, but
gives zero in the confining phase. Alternatively, one can use two parallel Wilson lines in the
fundamental representation of SU(2) winding along the same cycle. In the tensor product,
it contains a trivial representation and an adjoint, and hence distinguishes the two by giving
4 in theZ 2 phase and 1 in the confining phase.
Therefore,S(T 3)⊂H(T 3)∨ is a 9-dimensional space. Phrasing it in the dual picture,
any possible incoming states inH(T 3) will become a linear combination of the 10 states in
Hλ=[2]⊕H λ=[12] after mass deformation and RG flow, but they only define 9 independent
functionals onS(T 3) as the two confining vacua cannot be distinguished by Wilson lines. In
other words, we have

3.3.2SL(3)
As SL(2) is very special, before moving on to the most general case, we will analyze another
case withK= SU(3), which has an “intermediate vaccum” after deformation. In general,
such vacua won’t contribute to the partition function of the Vafa–Witten theory or the
Witten index due to the presence of fermionic zero modes, but they can contribute to the
skein module. Therefore, it is necessary for us to better understand and correctly incorporate
them.
TheZ 3/electric phase.The other two types of vacua for the SU(3) theory are pretty
similar to the SU(2) case. TheZ 3 vacuum associated with the irreducible embedding (prin-
cipal nilpotent orbit),λ= [3], will lead to 3 3 = 27 states onT 3 that can be distinguished
by 27 configurations ofZ 3 Wilson lines. Notice that in each direction, one needs both of
the non-trivialZ 3 Wilson lines, otherwise|ω 2⟩, whereωis the generator ofZ 3, cannot be
distinguished from a linear combination of|1⟩and|ω⟩.
The confinement/magnetic phase.For the trivialsl(2)-triple (zero orbit),λ= [1 3],
one has three confining vacua, which can’t be distinguished from each other via Wilson
lines. To distinguish them from the 27 states in theZ 3 phase, one can use three parallel
24

===== PAGE 26 =====
Wilson lines in the fundamental representation (equivalently, one can simply use the adjoint
or a pair of fundamental and anti-fundamental). As this tensor product is decomposed as
3×3×3=1+8+8+10, the vev of this configuration is 27 in theZ 3 phase but 1 in the
confinement phase. Therefore, the three different quantum vacua collectively contribute a
one-dimensional space to sk(T 3,SL(3)), but 3 to the Witten index.
The Maxwell/neutral phase.The intermediate vacuum corresponds to the embedding
ofsl(2) as a “2×2 block,” 


∗ ∗0
0 0 0

⊂sl(3).
This breaks the gauge group to a U(1) subgroup parametrized as diag{eiθ,eiθ,e−2iθ}. In this
phase, there is a massless vector multiplet, leading to both bosonic and fermionic zero modes.
The latter makes the contribution of such vacua to the partition function vanish, while the
former are parametrized by holonomies U(1) 3 along theT 3. A ground state wave function
is the constant function on U(1) 3, up to the action of fermionic zero modes. When a Wilson
line is inserted along a non-trivial cycle ofT 3, the correlation function is expected to be the
multiplicity of the trivial representation of U(1). This allows one to distinguish this vacuum
from the rest. For example, the fundamental of SU(3) does not have an invariant subspace
of this U(1) subgroup, but the adjoint does. Notice that all the first 27 configurations can-
not distinguish this vacuum|U(1)⟩with the superposition|ψ⟩ Z3 = (|1⟩+|ω⟩+|ω 2⟩)⊗3 or a
confining vacuum|c⟩. The 28th generator, taken to be a parallel pair of fundamental and
anti-fundamental (which can be viewed as a fundamental but with the opposite orientation),
equivalent to a sum of a trivial and adjoint line, evaluates to 1 on|c⟩, 9 on|ψ⟩ Z3, and 5
on|U(1)⟩, still cannot distinguish|U(1)⟩from a linear combination of|c⟩and|ψ⟩ Z3. There-
fore, an additional generator is really needed (which is also expected simply on dimensional
grounds). The 29th generator can be taken to be three parallel Wilson lines in the fun-
damental representation. This gives 1 on|c⟩, 12 on|U(1)⟩and 27 on|ψ⟩ Z3, which enables
to distinguish between all three of them when combined with the 28th generator. In the
above, we used the values of the adjoint8and the decuplet10on the three vacua, which is
summarized below:
|c⟩ |U(1)⟩ |ψ⟩Z3

10 0 3 10
They can themselves serve as the 28th and 29th generators, but we chose to use only the
fundamental in the description above which contains them in the tensor product.

In the end, we have the dimension for the skein module being 29 while the Witten index is
30. The difference is only one, but this is somewhat misleading—among the three difference
phases, only one gives the same contribution to both dim sk(T 3) and the index.
3.3.3PSL(2)andPSL(3)
WhenGis non-simply connected, there can be non-trivial magnetic sectors, and our proposal
is that the “total” skein module is the direct sum ofS m over all sectors, which we now apply
to study the non-simply-connected versions of the previous two examples, starting with
K= SO(3).
Asπ 1(G) =Z 2, there are 8 magnetic sectors labeled byH 2(T 3,Z 2) =Z 3
2. They form
two SL(3,Z)-orbits with one consisting ofm= 0 only while the other containing all the
non-trivialm’s. Therefore, we only need to find the contributions of each phase toS m with
m= 0 and another non-trivialm.
Electric phase (principal orbit).In this phase, the SO(3) gauge group will be com-
pletely broken by the vevs of the scalars withC λ = 0. The trivial connection can still
contribute tom= 0, but no configuration can contribute to a sector with non-trivialm.
One can also interpret this statement in the same phase of the SU(2) theory. There, one has
aZ 2 gauge theory, but there is not aZ 2-connection that can accommodate a magnetic flux.
This always happens to theories withKof adjoint type in an electric phase. (Recall that
the phase classification is uniform across groups with the same Lie algebra and previously
stated using the global form with simply-connectedH.)
Magnetic phase (zero orbit).In this phase, the SO(3) gauge group is confined, and,
similar to the SU(2) case, the two quantum vacua are not distinguishable by Wilson lines.
Therefore, it contributes a one-dimensional space toS m for anym. Another way of under-
standing this is via the low-energy theory which consists of two quantum vacua corresponding
to the condensation of either a dyon or a charge-2 monopole. In them= 0 sector, the two
are indistinguishable by Wilson lines which are confined, and are exchanged under a shift of
the theta angle by 2π. The monopole condensation leaves unbroken aZ 2 magnetic 1-form
symmetry, which can be viewed as the center symmetry of a magneticZ 2 gauge theory with
background being exactlym. It then contributes a one-dimensional space toS m for all values
ofm.
Therefore, we again have
dim sk(T3,SO(3)) = 1 + 8 = 9.(3.13)

===== PAGE 28 =====

Here the contributions of the non-trivial electric- and magnetic-flux sectors are colored blue

partition and switch the electric flux with a magnetic flux. This turns out to be how the
Langlands duality forT 3 skein module in theA-series works in general.

The only additional analysis needed is about the abelian phase labeled byλ= [21] in the
presence of a non-trivial magnetic flux. There, the U(1) gauge group is required to have a
non-trivial flux with the first Chern class congruent tom(mod 3). This makes the ground-
state energy in such a sector non-zero. It is therefore not visible at low energy and cannot
contribute to the skein module.
Also, in such tables with contributions of phases to different global forms ofKlisted,
it is fairly easy to tell which phase is electric (contributing to the non-trivial electric-flux
sectors for the simply-connected group but not to these with non-trivial magnetic flux for the
adjoint-type group), magnetic (the exact opposite), neutral (only contributing to the trivial
sector regardless of the global form), or hybrid (contributing to both non-trivial electric and
magnetic sectors).
3.4 GeneralG
For a general reductiveG, the procedure is similar. Whenπ 1(G) is non-trivial, one needs
to again remember the magnetic flux sectors (i.e. different topological type of bundles). If
π1(G) has a free part, it allows non-trivial first Chern class for the gauge bundle to be turned
on. However, to have a flat connection (and hence visible at low energy and contribute to
the skein module),c 1 has to be torsion. Since we will be dealing withM 3 of the form
Σ×S 1—whose cohomology groups overZare free—in this section and the next, this forces
27

===== PAGE 29 =====
the Chern class to be trivial. As a consequence, only the torsion part ofπ 1(G) is relevant,
and the magnetic fluxes will be classified bym∈H 2(M3,Torπ 1(G)).
Turning on the mass deformation, one again has a collection of IR phases, classified
by the conjugacy classλof thesl 2-triple ing, and, for eachλ, one needs to study the
vacuum structure of the 4dN= 1 theory with gauge groupK λ whose complexification
Gλ is the centralizer of the image ofλunder the adjoint action ofG. In general,K λ has
a connected pieceK 0

parts ofK λ separately and then putting them together across the different phases.
The discrete/free part
For the zero-flux sectorm= 0, one can first count flatC λ-connections overM 3 to find

The continuous/confined part
For each suchλ, one expects confinement to make the non-abelian partH λ ofK 0

of dual Coxeter numbers for the simple summands of Lie(Gλ)—but different quantum vacua
are related by shifts of theta angles and are mutually indistinguishable by Wilson lines.
Therefore, one only needs to take into account the contribution of a single vacuum. The
low-energy theory at each vacuum is an abelian gauge theory, the continuous part of which

Summing over the phases
There is a remaining subtlety before one can straightforwardly combine contributions from
all phases: one still needs to argue that all of them are distinguishable by Wilson lines
labeled by representations ofK. It is not too hard to convince oneself that, after fixing the
phaseλ, all connections inM m(T 3,Cλ)—or all components inM m(T 3,K IR
λ ) if there are
more in the latter—can be distinguished by Wilson lines, based on the fact that they give
a complete basis for class functions forK, and hence are expected to distinguish connected
components of (conjugacy classes of) holonomy representationsπ 1(M3)→K IR
λ .19 However,
it is not immediately clear that each flat connection can always be distinguished from other
ones indifferentphases. We will see that this, in general, does fail, and summing up the
number of flat connections in all phases could result in an overcount.
Because of this subtlety and the one that involves the difference between flatK IR

3.5 More examples
We now analyze additional cases in varying depth, covering all types of simple Lie algebras.
We will start with theA-series where none of the two subtleties arise.

Notice that for the SU(N) computation, the sum is over phases, as each vacuum in the same

On the PSU(N) side, we are reorganizing the vacua not according to the phases where they
come from, but their remaining magnetic symmetry. This is not just about taking the
transpose of the Young diagrams and quite different from the duality of the skein module.
In general, the computations forIandSand their behavior are quite different. While
theS-duality ofN= 4 theories combined with the invariance of the Vafa–Witten partition
function under theN= 1 deformation always gives
I(T 3,G) =I(T 3,LG),(3.28)
it is not as simple for the dimension ofScomputed from the deformation, due to the various
subtleties involved. Our focus will be on the latter in the rest of the paper, and we only
include the index calculation when it serves to illustrate certain phenomena forS. To explain
the duality forSfrom theS-duality of theN= 4 theory, one should perform a computation
for eSand argue that the subspaces added by incorporating ’t Hooft and dyonic operators
are isomorphic on the two side.
One can test the duality for eSwith the simplifying assumption that, once all dyonic
operators are added, one can distinguish between the quantum vacua. The computation is

largely a combination of the skein one and the index one. WhenN=pis prime, all the
intermediate phases still contribute equally to both the SU and PSU sides. For the partition
[1p], one still addp−1 states on both side in thee=m= 0 sector, as the magneticZ p
symmetry on the PSU(p) side is only supported on one of the quantum vacuum. Therefore,
adding or removing the ’t Hooft operators would add or remove an isomorphic subspace on
the two sides. This observation, combined with theS-duality of eS, leads to the Langlands
duality forSforA p−1. WhenNis not a prime, the analysis will be slightly more complicated,
and the verification of duality would requires reorganizing the quantum vacua similar to the
Witten index computation. We will not perform this calculation here in full generality, but
only in theA 3 example—disguised asD 3—in a later subsection.
We will next explore simple groups ofB- andC-types, where we will encounter several
complications not present for theA-series, such as the presence of the exceptional hybrid
phases. In particular, they make it difficult to match the skein modules of the Langlands
dual groups.
3.5.2B- andC-series
AsB- andC-type groups are Langlands/GNO dual, we will study them together. For both,
the two subtleties—the appearance of O(2) theories in the IR and some flat connections being
“not independent”—arise, and one of our focus is to illustrate their effects in examples.
A first look atG= Sp(2N).The nilpotent orbits—or phases of the deformed theory—are
again labeled by partitionsλ⊢2Nbut now with the condition that odd numbers appear
even number of times. In other words,r i≡0 (mod 2) for any oddi. The unbroken gauge
group in the phaseλis given by 23

and a reflection whenr i is odd. This is the starting point for the phase classification:
•A phase is electric unless all even parts inλhave even multiplicity. Then the center
Z2 acts as a reflection on some O(ri) factors and becomes part ofC λ. This includes [4]
and [212] forN= 2.
23We have used the convention that the compact part of Sp(2N,C) is denoted as Sp(N), with Sp(1)≃

35

===== PAGE 37 =====
•The phase is magnetic if all even parts have even multiplicities not equal to two. Notices

Sp(N) will be the diagonal of the center of Sp(r odd) groups and becomes confined in
the IR. ForN= 4, they includeλ= [3 212],[2 4],and [1 8].
•The phase is of type HE if some even part has multiplicity two. Then the infrared
dynamics is given by a product of O(2) andZ 2 gauge theories. Notice that being HE
and E are not mutually exclusive in the Sp case. ForN= 4, there are three HE phases,
[42], [2214] and [42 2], with the last also being electric (hence E-HE).
On the other hand, neutral phases cannot be present in the Sp cases.

Counting O(2)-connections onT 3.One main difference between O(2) and its compo-
nent groupZ 2 is that a reflectionSin O(2) commutes with aπ-rotationR, as well as another
reflectionR′ =SR. Together with the identityI, they form aZ 2×Z 2 subgroup. However,
counting flat O(2)-connections also differs from countingZ 2×Z 2 ones, asRandR ′ are
conjugate. We organize the flat connections in terms of the number of reflections involved
along the three 1-cycles ofT 3:
•No reflection: There is a single component parametrized by SO(2) holonomies modulo
conjugation.
•One reflection: The other two holonomies will have to beIorR, resulting in 2 2·3 = 12
components.
•Two reflections: Both reflections can beS, or one isSwhile the other isS ′, giving
2·3 = 6 choices; for each option, the remaining holonomy will have to beIorR, giving
again 12 components.
36

===== PAGE 38 =====
•Three reflections: Once a chosen reflection is fixed to beS, the other two can be either
SorS ′, giving 2 2 = 4 components.
Summing them up, there are 29 components in total.
Depending on how the O(2) embeds into the UV gauge groupK, it can have either
electric or magnetic symmetry contributing to different flux sectors.24 The electric symmetry
is associated with the action ofH 1(M3,Z 2) on the moduli space (and the induced action
on the space of locally constant functions). In the present case ofM 3 =T 3, the action of
H1(M3,Z 2)≃Z 3
2 can permute different components, but in a way that preserves the number
of reflections. It is easy to see that for components with 1, 2, or 3 reflections, they are
organized into orbits of size 4, with aZ 2 acting trivially while aZ 2×Z 2 acting transitively.
Therefore, the space of functions split as 1+3 as characters ofZ 3
2 (i.e. a trivial character and
three non-trivial ones). Summing them up, one has the total decomposition 29 = 8 + 3×7
with an 8-dimensionalZ 3
2-invariant subspace and a three-dimensional space for each of the
seven non-trivial characters.
If one gauges the electricZ 2 1-form symmetry, the resulting theory still has an O(2)
gauge group—although, in a sense, with smaller radius—but it will be “a magnetic O(2)”
subgroup ofK/Z 2 and contributes to different magnetic flux sectors. For this purpose, one
grades the components byH 2(M3,Z 2) using the second Stiefel–Whitney classes of the O(2)
bundle. The classw 2 will be non-trivial along a plane if the two holonomies are distinct
in the non-trivial part ofZ 2×Z 2⊂O(2). One then has exactly the same decomposition
29 = 8 + 3×7. A quick way to see the 8 is the following. Whenw 2 = 0, all the reflections
have to be the same and the rest has to be the identity (unless there is no reflection, but
then they are all in the same connected component). So one can simply count 2 3 = 8. Using
SL(3,Z) invariance, we only need to explain the 3 for a givenw 2. Choosing it to be (1,0,0),
we can enumerate the three components to be (1,S,S ′), (1,S,R) and (1,R,S).
We are now better equipped to understand theT 3 skein module forC 2≃Sp(4,C).

Notice that for the equality of the index, the difference in the number of quantum vacua
in different phases make up for the fact that there are two electric phases while only one
magnetic phase. However, for the skein module, we are out of luck as the quantum vacua
are indistinguishable by Wilson lines.
There are several possible interpretations for the apparent mismatch:
•There are overlaps on the Sp(2) side, meaning that the five states in ane̸= 0 sector that
we counted actually satisfy a linear relation. Someone who believes in this possibility
might be encouraged by the observation that the flatZ 2-connections in theλ= [4]
phase all embed into the continuous component of the O(2) moduli space and, therefore,
maybe they can “tunnel” into each other and should only be counted as one quantum
state. However, there are further problems when comparing Sp and SO for higher
ranks, and it seems that one needs an increasingly long list of ad-hoc rules to maintain
the Langlands duality between them.
25TheS-dual of the Sp(2) theory is the SO(5) + theory with a trivial discrete theta angle. Unlike the
SO(3) case, it will not become the SO(5) − theory whenθ7→θ+ 2π. See [44] for more details on such pairs
of theories. In this work, we only discuss the SO(N) + theories and will omit the subscript, but the existence
of the SO(N)− theory would indicate that there is a different kind of skein module for SO(N), which would
be interesting to explore.
38

===== PAGE 40 =====
•The skein modules actually match, but our method of computing it via theN= 1
deformation has certain subtleties. This is a logical possibility and it seems that the
mismatch is always associated with HE phases where O(2) or a higher-rank analogue
appears. It would be interesting to understand more precisely the physics of the de-
formation such as the detailed behavior of the states⟨L|in the Hilbert space.
•The duality between skein modules for Langlands dual groups actually fails forB–
Cpairs. This interpretation is preferred by the author, as physics only predicts a
duality for eS, while the one forSis only expected to hold when ignoring ’t Hooft and
dyonic operators removes isomorphic subspaces on the two sides. If this is the correct
interpretation, one should be able to remedy the duality by adding back the “missing”
line operators. Indeed, assuming that the quantum vacua can now be distinguished
with dyonic operators, we have an increase of dimensions of 1 + 7 vs 1 for the [21 2]
electric phase on the two sides, but 2 vs 2×(1 + 7) for the [1 4] magnetic phase.
Therefore, one has dim eS= 56 = 14 + 42 on both sides. 26
Although the author prefers the third interpretation, we will not take an official stance, 27
but instead proceed to analyze some higher-rank cases, pinpoint the source for mismatches,
and give our best attempt to restore duality first without invoking ’t Hooft operators. It
turns out that many phases match perfectly, and the problem seems to be always associated
with hybrid phases, which perhaps shows that the first and the second interpretations also
have some merits.
We now discuss some general aspects of theB-series (which we didn’t have to use for the
computation in the SO(5) case above due to the exceptional isomorphism), starting with the
non-simply-connected SO groups.
Phase structures forB N.To label nilpotent orbits inB N, the partitionλis required
to have even multiplicity for all (non-zero) even numbers,r i≡0 (mod 2) for all eveni.
26Notice that a difference between the quantum vacua of SO(5) and SO(3) plays a crucial role: for the
SO(5) theory, each of the three quantum vacua has the magneticZ 2 symmetry, while for SO(3) only one
of them has the magnetic symmetry. Therefore, for the SU(2) vs SO(3) pair, removing ’t Hooft operators
from eSdoes not create a mismatch forS, but it does for the Sp(2)–SO(5) pair. There are a few closely
related perspectives on this difference: 1) whether a theory has oblique confinement or not, 2) the existence
of “fractional” instantons in the theory, 3) whether the “spectral flow” in the sense of [28] involves fractional
coefficients, 4) the relation between the (Pontryagin) square of thew 2(G) and thep 1(G) of the gauge bundle,
and 5) the Postnokovk-invariant of BG.
27Including more general line operators can lead to various complications in the analysis, and treating them
systematically is beyond the scope of this work. So far our discussion of them mostly involves separating
different quantum vacua in the same phase, and it would be interesting to understand whether they also
play other roles, such as making certain indistinguishable electric phases distinguishable.

For the Spin group,K λ is a spin double cover of the one above. This would not increase
Cλ unless allr i = 1 for oddi, for which the partition is referred as “rather odd.” Therefore,
the phase classification is as follows:
•Whenλis rather odd, the phase will be electric. TheZ 2 center of Spin(2N+ 1) is part
of the discrete gauge symmetry in the IR. For the SO(5) case, such phases are [5] and
[221].
•When one of the oddihas multiplicity larger than 2, we have a magnetic phase. The
center of Spin(2N+ 1) is inside the double cover of S ( Q O(ri)) with the product over
ri >2 with oddiand becomes confined in the IR. This allows turning on magnetic
fluxes in the SO(2N+ 1) theory. For SO(5), the only such phase isλ= [1 5].
•Whenr i = 2 for one or more oddi, it is an HE phase. The IR theory will be a product
of O(2) and (if any)Z 2 gauge theories. For the SO(5) case,λ= [31 2] gives such a
phase. Notice that an HE phase can also be magnetic (hence M-HE) in theB-series,
dual to the E-HE phases inC-series. One example isλ= [3 213] for SO(9). For such a
phase, the center doesn’t completely disappear in the IR, but one can still turn on a
flux via the O(2) theory.
Furthermore, it is not possible to have an HO phase, asZ 2 has no non-trivial proper sub-
groups, or a neutral phase, as it would require only having a single pair of odd parts with
ri = 2 (which will become possible for groups ofD-type).
Now we are prepared to test the duality betweenB 3 andC 3.

Notice that there is again a mismatch, either for the Sp(3)–SO(7) pair or for the Sp(3)/Z2–
Spin(7) pair. One possible way to remedy this mismatch is to identify a certain overlap
phenomenon on the Spin/SO(7) side as well. A candidate for such an overlap is highlighted
in orange. What is special about thisλ= [3 21] phase is that theπ-rotation of its O(2)
coincides with a reflection of the O(2) gauge group of theλ= [51 1] phase. Furthermore, for
Spin(7), it overlaps with theλ= [7] phase, in a manner similar to what we observed in the
C2 case. If we take this observation as a blessing, allowing us to reduce the contributions in
orange to 1 for Spin(7) and 1 + 7 for SO(7), then the duality could be restored.
I hope the reader would agree with the author that haphazardly introducing new rules
of this kind is not particularly aesthetically pleasing. It might be therefore preferable to
accept that the skein modules are not dual by themselves, and that one should consider the
enriched version eSincorporating ’t Hooft and dyonic operators to have the duality restored.
We will not demonstrate the full duality for eShere, but note that one would obtain an

the remaining contributions match in a fashion similar to the Witten index (to which the
HE phases do not contribute), this yields a duality for the dimensions of eS.28

•The rather odd partitionλ SO = [531] can actually contribute to sectors with a non-
trivial magnetic background, due to a subtlety we mentioned before involving non-
abelian central extensions. In SO(9), the three non-trivial elements of theZ 2×Z 2
gauge group can be represented as diagonal matrices with±1 entries. They can be
chosen to have respectively 4,6 and 8 negative entries. They commute in SO(9),
but after lifting to Spin(9), they are represented by product of gamma matrices as
a=γ 1234,b=γ 456789 andc=ab=γ 12356789. They satisfya 2 =c 2 = 1,b 2 =−1,
and any pair anti-commutes. They now generate a non-abelian extension ofZ 2×Z 2
that is isomorphic to the order-8 dihedral group D 8. To havew 2(SO(9)) = 0, the three
holonomies can only contain one of the three non-trivial elements ofZ 2×Z 2, giving
22 = 1 + 3·7 choices. For each non-trivialw 2, there are 6 choices. To see this, one
can use the SL(3,Z) symmetry to setw 2 = (1,0,0). Then the 6 = 3×2 choices of
holonomies are of the form (e,x,y) withxandybeing distinct elements of{a,b,c}.
•On theC 4 side, after substituting the indistinguishable phases with some of the non-
special ones, there is an additional electric phaseλ= [41 4] that doesn’t have a dual
magnetic phase. Notice that for this phase, the flatZ 2-connections are embedded in
the O(2) moduli space of the [2 214] phase. This is analogous to some of the previous
cases, and if we perform a similar modification and eliminate the contributions to the
e̸= 0 sectors, then it can be pair with one of the remaining non-special electric phase
on the SO(9) side.
After this, the only mismatch concerns the rather odd partitionλ SO = [531], which again
seems to be related to an overlap with an HE phase, now the one given byλ= [711]. Indeed,
a generator ofZ 2×Z 2 with eight−1 and one +1 on the diagonal is a reflection in the O(2)
gauge group for the [711] phase. If it is killed by this overlap, the gauge group for the [531]
phase will be reduced toZ 2, which gives an 8-dimensional space for (the dual of)S, matching
exactly theλ= [2 4] phase on the Sp(4) side. After these modifications, both sides will sum
up to be 453 = 75 + 7·54.
This example concludes our discussion of theB- andC-type groups. We will now turn to
theD-series, where we will have less trouble verifying the Langlands duality. This (hopefully)
suggests that the difficulties encountered for theB–Cpairs reflect a genuine mismatch, rather
than a lack of skill or effort on the author’s part.
3.5.3D-series
For theD-series, the simply-connected global form is Spin(2N), whose center isZ 4 for odd
NandZ 2×Z 2 for evenN. The classification of phases is similar to theB-series, except
43

===== PAGE 45 =====
that for “very even” partitions (only even numbers appear in the partition which can only
happen for evenN) there are two nilpotent orbits hence two phases.
ForK= SO(2N), the unbroken gauge group in a phaseλlabeled by the partition
[1r12r2...] is

.(3.35)
For Spin(2N), one can simply take its spin double cover. So far, this is almost identical to
the SO(2N+ 1) case, but now we also have two additional global forms, the semi-Spin group
Ss(2N) and the adjoint-type group PSO(2N). For PSO(2N), the gauge group in each phase
can be obtain from the above by modding out the diagonal±1action, and one can then
take a double cover to obtain the gauge group associated with the semi-Spin group. The
phase classification is also analogous to theB-series, except that N- and HO-type of phases
are now possible:
•Whenλis rather odd (each odd part appearing exactly once), the phase will be electric,
and the full center of Spin(2N) becomes part of the discrete gauge symmetry in the
IR. For the SO(8) case, such phases are [71], [53] and [32 21].
•When all of the oddihas multiplicity larger than 2, we get a magnetic phase. The
full center of Spin(2N) is inside the double cover of S (Q O(ri)) with the product over
ri >2 with oddiand becomes confined in the IR.
•For ordinary hybrid phases, they can arise when some odd parts appear only once
while some others appear with multiplicity larger than 2. Alternatively, they also arise
for very even partitions. For these phases, aZ 2 subgroup of the center is inH λ while
aZ 2 quotient is inC λ. Examples includeλ= [2 2],[4 2],[51 3],[31 5], etc.
•When there is exactly one oddiwhich also hasr i = 2, one gets a neutral phase. This
is only possible whenNis odd. The IR theory has a SO(r i)≃U(1) gauge field, and
theZ 4 center of the Spin(2N) theory is fully contained in it.
•When one oddihasr i = 2 and there are other odd parts, one gets an HE phase. The
smallest example isλ= [3 212], which is a rather special one withK λ = S(O(2)×O(2))
which we have to deal with in theD 4 case.
We will work through some low-rank cases untilD 4, starting withD 2≃A 1⊕A 1, which
already demonstrates the “very even” phenomenon unique to theD-series.

The HO phaseλ SO = [31 3] will “split off” an additional quantum vacuum previously un-
recognizable by the Wilson lines. It does not have a magnetic gauge symmetry, but its
contribution is multiplied by the number of the “electric”C λ flat connections. For the neu-
tral phaseλ SO = [2211], there will be another neutral vacuum now being detectable. Lastly,
for the magnetic phase, there will be three additional quantum vacua, one of which will carry
anZ 2 magnetic gauge symmetry for both PSO and SO. Summing over their contributions,
we see a duality between the Spin–PSO pair, as well as the self-duality of the SO(6) case.
The previous examples might give the false impression that the Langlands duality also
holds automatically forD N in a simple manner. We will demonstrate with theN= 4 case
that, while the duality is still true, the realization is more subtle.

We give multiple remarks about the table above:
•The phase [53] always “overlaps” with [71] and is indistinguishable from it by Wilson
lines, as the holonomies are both in the center ofG. Therefore, one should only count
one of them.
•This spoils the Lustzig–Spaltenstein duality between the special orbits in the sense
that, although dual orbits still have the same contribution, the overlap patterns are in
general different. For example, on the opposite end, there is no problem distinguishing
the two phases labeled by [2 214] and [1 8] using confinement patterns.
•The non-special orbit [32 21] (in orange) plays a crucial role in restoring the Langlands
duality for skein modules to make up for the “lost part” due to the overlap between
the [53] and [71] phases. It ensures that there are the same number of electric phases
as magnetic phases and they can be paired together.
•Forλ= [2 214], the groupK λ = SU(2)3⊂Spin(8) has the action of the centerZ 2×Z 2
that flips even copies of SU(2)’s. The quotient by any of the threeZ 2 subgroups
is identified with SU(2)×SO(4), although with three different SO(4)’s. The quotient
SU(2)3/Z2
2 inside PSO(8) can be thought of in a triality-invariant way as theZ2 central
extension of SO(3)3 in a way symmetric under permutation. Thisλcorresponds to the
minimal nilpotent orbit, whose weighted Dynkin diagram has only a non-zero label for
the central node.
•There are HO phases that are not triality invariant but instead form size-3 orbits.
There are two such groups and they both consist of two very even partitions and an
additional one. To restore triality, one can include the Sc(8) theory by swapping the
very evenλ’s in the Ss(8) theory. Then the SO-Ss-Sc theories form a “triality triangle.”
47

===== PAGE 49 =====
•For the HE phase labeled byλ= [3 212], we haveK λ being S(O(2)×O(2)), which
is perhaps the most complicated one we have encountered so far. One can obtain
its moduli space of flat connections by “gluing together” two O(2) moduli spaces by
requiring that the reflections for the two copies are always paired up. For each chosen
flat connection for O(2) 1 with a non-zero number of reflections, one always has four
choices for the holonomies of the second copy, as one can conjugate a reflection to a
standard one while the other two holonomies can each be flipped. Then we see that the
previous 29 = 8+21 = 1+7×(1+3) now becomes 113 = 8+105 = 1+7×(1+3)×(1+3).
One can then argue that the refinement is simply about “coloring the 3’s.” The fact
that S(O(2)×O(2)) can be electric, magnetic or mixed is important in ensuring the
Langlands duality. If one throws away the continuous part and just work with the
component groupC λ =Z 2, it will be difficult for the duality to work.
Although these examples suggest that theD-series is better behaved under Langlands
duality, we will not try to go beyondD 4 in the present work but instead turn our attention
to the exceptional groups for the remainder of this section.
3.5.4 Exceptional cases
ForG 2 andF 4, there is no non-trivial check of the duality, and our main job will be to
correctly putting together different phases and try to avoid overcounts.

One can attempt to remedy this mismatch by arguing that theD 4 magnetic phase is
also indistinguishable. In general, the overlapping phenomenon often happens when the
same Bala–Carter Levi has several distinguished orbits (i.e. multiple orbits share the same
minimal Levi). ForE 6, the phases labeled byλ=E 6, E6(a1) andD 5 are expected to
32One can similarly defineN n :=N(T An)≃U(1) n ⋊Sn+1 the normalizer of the Cartan ofA n. ThenN 1 =
