===== PAGE 102 =====
6 Discussion
In the last section of the paper, we will first discuss new ways to look at some earlier results
through the lens of our construction, and then mention several interesting future directions.
6.1 New perspectives
On the Gilmer–Masbaum map.In [18], the authors use SO(3) WRT invariants to
establish a lower bound on the dimension of the Kauffman-bracket skein module for Σ×S 1.
Rephrased in our setup, their work can be viewed as choosing the test state|ψ⟩in the
Hilbert spaceH(M 3) to be a “Chern–Simons state”|WRT r,s⟩, labeled by a root of unity
eiπs/r specified by a pair of integersrands, which then sends a class in the skein module
to its WRT invariant. In other words, a natural class of states in the quantum mechanics is
given by flat connections onM 3, and an appropriate linear combination of them gives the
WRT invariant once paired with⟨L|[81, 82, 20]. The linear combination depends on (r,s),
and the Gilmer–Masbaum evaluation map can be interpreted as using such a family of test
states to distinguish different skein classes.
Relation tobZ.Another set of natural linear combinations is related to the bZa invariant [22,
23], which, rather than being specified by a root of unity, is labeled bya∈TorH 1(M3,Q(g))/W.
These labels can be thought of asS-dual to abelian flat connections and are related to spin c
structures forG= SL(2) [83] (see also [30, Sec. 5.7] for a recent discussion of the nature of
thealabel). Therefore, they also define evaluation maps on skein classes,
⟨L|a⟩⇝ bZa(M3,L),(6.1)
giving the generalization of the bZa invariants to the pair (M3,L) studied in [23, Sec. 4]. This
set of states labeled byaseems to have a more natural interplay with skein modules, asq
can take arbitrary values, unlike the WRT case where it is restricted to be a root of unity.
This may provide a productive framework to study both bZand skein modules. 54
Unifying homological invariants of 3-manifolds.At this point, it might be illumi-
nating to compare several vector-space-valued invariants associated with 3-manifolds and
discuss how they are related to each other through the two-dimensionalT[M 3×S 1] theory.
54An interesting work in this direction, which appeared around the same time as the first version of the
present paper, is [84].
101

===== PAGE 103 =====
•Khovanov homology.The Khovanov homology [85] and its generalizations (e.g., [86–
89,20,22,23]) can be obtained from the BPS Hilbert space of theT[M 3×S 1] theory on
an interval (or half-line) with a special “tip-of-the-cigar” boundary condition given by
the shrinking of the internalS 1. WhenM 3 =S 3, there is a canonical choice of the other
boundary condition given by the unique flat connection onS 3. For more generalM 3,
a natural set of boundary conditions is given by thea-labels for bZ. Links inM 3 can be
incorporated by either codimension-two or codimension-four defects in the 6d theory,
leading to operators that are either space-filling from the 2d perspective or localized
along the tip-of-the-cigar boundary condition, both of which modify the Hilbert space.
The latter approach is closely related to our construction upon compactifying the time
circle, while viewing the interval direction as the new time direction.
•Hilbert spaces of the bZa-TQFT.As bZis expected to define a 3d TQFT (though
it can only satisfy a generalized version of the Atiyah–Segal axioms), one can study
its cutting-and-gluing behavior along Riemann surfaces. The Hilbert spaces onT 2
and higher-genus surfaces have been studied in [83, 90, 91]. Using the functoriality of
compactification, these spaces are expected to be related to the Khovanov homology
of Σ×S 1 discussed in the previous point. It would be interesting to compare them
and make this connection more precise.
•Floer-like homologies.The instanton Floer homology [92] and its variants and gen-
eralizations are related to Hilbert spaces of 4dN= 2 theories on 3-manifolds with the
Donaldson–Witten twist [93]. One way to construct such theories is by compactifying
the 6d (2,0) theory on a sphere with two irregular singularities [94]. As this geome-
try can be viewed as anS 1-fibration over an interval, one should be able to recover
the Floer homology from theT[M 3×S 1] theory on an interval with two boundary
conditions similar to the tip-of-the-cigar one but with poles/singularities.
•Vafa–Witten homology.The Hilbert spaceH(M 3) of the Vafa–Witten theory on
M3 is closely related to the BPS Hilbert spaceH T[M 3×S1](S1) ofT[M 3×S 1] onS 1.
They are not identical due to the difference between the 4dN= 4 theory and the
T[T 2] theory. AsH(M 3) can be recovered from the larger space by a “moduli-space
flow” (i.e. zooming in to the neighborhood of an SCFT singularity and flowing to the
IR), one might expect thatH(M 3) is a quotient ofH T[M 3×S1](S1), though it is not
entirely clear whether any BPS states of theN= 4 theory onM 3 can be extended
to the 6d theory onM 3×T 2 (i.e. whether there are “emergent states” inH(M 3)). It
would be interesting to better understand the exact relation between the two Hilbert
spaces.
•Skein module.Using our construction, the skein module ofM 3 is embedded into
102

===== PAGE 104 =====
the dual of the Vafa–Witten homologyH(M 3)∨. However, as the construction can be
uplifted to 6d, we expect that it can be further embedded intoH T[M 3×S1](S1)∨. In
other words,Sdoes not contain “emergent states” since any⟨L|can be lifted to the
6d theory. One interesting consequence is that the TQFT structure of the 2d theory
might lead to a non-trivial interplay between skein modules and Floer-like homologies
via the open-closed map. More concretely, any state in the open Hilbert space on an
interval with two boundary conditionsBandB ∨ can propagate to a state in theS 1
Hilbert space, becoming a functional on skein classes inS. This connection between
the open and closed sectors of the 2d TQFT is also related to the next point.
On skein-valued curve counting.In the work of Ekholm and Shende [95], it was argued
that the counting of holomorphic curves with boundaries on a LagrangianM3 in a Calabi–Yau
three-foldXis naturally valued in the HOMFLY-PT skein module ofM 3. If one truncates
to the SL(N)-skein module, then the M-theory lift of our construction provides a natural
physics realization for their work. The M-theory setup is
Nfivebranes:R×M 3×“cigar”
∩ ∩
space-time:R×X×Taub–NUT
∪
∈
M2-brane:R×C×O
(6.2)
with the condition thatCis a holomorphic curve with∂C⊂M 3. Here, “cigar” is topo-
logically anR 2 subspace of the Taub–NUT space, but should be better thought of as a
circle-fibration overR + with a smooth tip and approaching constant size at infinity. So far,
this is exactly the same as the M-theory definition of bZ, which counts BPS states given by
such M2-branes. To relate to our construction, we compactify the firstRfactor into a circle
and make it—as well as the fiberS 1 of the cigar—small compared to the size of the rest of
the geometry. This leads to almost the same system used in our construction with a small
difference—the 4d theory is now the “T[T 2] theory,” containing the super–Yang–Mills theory
as a subsector at low energy that we can restrict ourselves to. An M2-brane wrappingS 1×C
with∂C=γ⊂M 3 then generates a state⟨γ|withγcolored by the defining representations
of SL(N), naturally living inS⊂H(M 3)∨. One can also fixγ, and consider the superposition
of all possible configurations of M2-branes ending onM 3 alongγ. This “Hartle–Hawking”
state is then #·⟨γ|with the coefficient counting such holomorphic curves. The invariance
of this state under skein relations then follows from the choice of the boundary condition of
the 4d theory atx= 0. It would be interesting to better understand this physical system to
give interpretations for some of the mysterious features of [95], such as certain phase factors
dependent on a choice of a 4-chain boundingM 3 inX.
103

===== PAGE 105 =====
6.2 Future directions
We have already mentioned several interesting directions to explore, ranging from a better
understanding of the physical details of the construction 55 to testing Langlands duality for
B–Cpairs and to comparisons with the geometry of the moduli spaces of Higgs bundles. We
will suggest several additional interesting problems for future exploration below.
The dual computation.As expected fromS-duality, one should be able to reproduce our
results in the dual theory with gauge groupLKand with insertions of ’t Hooft operators along
a Nahm-pole boundary condition atx= 0. One can also consider this boundary condition,
as well as its (p,q)-generalizations, on the original side with gauge groupK, which would
be needed for the enriched skein module eS. One starting point for this computation is to
determine whether such a boundary condition is compatible with theN= 1 deformation in
the bulk (or an analogous one) that we used, so as to allow the system to be studied in a
similar manner as a collection of different IR phases whenM 3 has reduced holonomy.
Skein algebra and category.One omission in this work is the treatment of skein algebras
and skein categories [98] associated with a Riemann surface Σ. To study these, one can simply
replace the circle factor with either the intervalIorR, which still allows one to turn on the
N= 1 deformation. A natural place to start is Σ =T 2, for which cosmic strings are not
present after the deformation. It would be interesting to reproduce the description of the
skein categories in [17] from boundary conditions of the 2d theory, which should be closely
related to the category of branes andC ∗-fixed points on the target of the 2d theory viewed
as a sigma model (cf. [11]).
More general three-manifolds.As theN= 1 deformation requires a K¨ ahler structure,
our focus in this paper is mostly on the case ofM 3 = Σ×S 1. However, there are many
interesting results about the compactification of 4d or even higher-dimensional theories on
various three-manifolds (see [99–107] for a small sample). One potentially useful perspective
is to consider the 2d (2,2)T[M 3×S 1] theory obtained from compactifying 5dN= 2
super–Yang–Mills theories onM 3. Such theories in the IR are often described (possibly after
deformations) by a twisted effective superpotential with finitely many discrete critical points
and the computation can simply reduce to counting them, perhaps with multiplicities and
55For example, in this paper, we do not fully justify the invariance ofSunder the deformation and RG
flow. This is a point that merits further investigation. In particular, it would be interesting to see how the
ghost number argument can be utilized to impose non-trivial constraints on the behavior of the BPS Hilbert
space under the RG flow. See also [96, 97] for related studies on the relation between line operators in the
UV and IR.
104

===== PAGE 106 =====
some equivalence relations.56 Some three-manifolds that are “one step away” from Σ×S1 are
circle bundles over Σ and mapping tori. For the latter, it would be interesting to connect the
physics understanding of theT[M 3] theory in [107] with recent results on the skein modules of
such three-manifold in [110]. Another potentially interesting way to deal with more general
M3 is to use the fact that every oriented smooth 4-manifold can be decomposed into two
K¨ ahler manifolds [111]. IfM3×Rcan be similarly decomposed, one can apply theN= 1
deformation on the two parts and glue together the skein modules.
Torsion in sk(M 3).Another interesting question concerns detecting torsion elements in
the skein module. Naively, this seems to be a blind spot in our approach, as torsion should
be related to jumps whenqis a root of unity, but the information aboutqseems to disappear
once we flow to the infrared phases after the deformation. To access roots of unity, one needs
to consider strong-coupling limits with Imτ→0. It would be interesting to understand
whether theN= 1 deformation can still be exploited, even in this limit, to give control over
torsion in the skein module of Σ×S 1.57 Another way to study such limits is by working in
the weakly coupledS-dual frame. Moreover, at special values of the coupling constant of
the UV theory, there might be higher-group/non-invertible symmetries or special types of
defects, which could also help to understand the torsion part of the module (cf. Footnote 34).
Large-Nlimit and gravity dual.WhenM 3 isS 3 or a lens spaceL(k,1), the large-N
limit of Chern–Simons theory can be described by a topological string theory on a local
Calabi–Yau threefold obtained fromT ∗M3 via the “conifold transition” [112–114]. Although
it is not clear how this process can be carried out for more generalM 3, there are hints
that the systems we considered, either in four dimensions or higher, have well-defined and
interesting large-Nlimits. For example, the skein-valued curve counting of [95] concerns
HOMFLY-PT skein modules, and the skein relations can be explained from the perspective
of topological strings [115]. On the other hand, theN= 1 deformation of the maximally
supersymmetric 4d Yang–Mills theory does have a well-studied string-theory dual due to
Polchinski and Strassler [116], and it would be interesting to see whether such an embedding
into string theory sheds further light on the properties of skein modules, skein algebras, and
skein categories.
56One example where this can be carried out is whenM 3 =L(k,1), and the skein module can indeed be
viewed as the space of functions on the massive vacua of the 2d theory. This system can be understood from
various angles. See [31, 108, 109, 53]. One feature that arises when the 2d theory has only massive vacua is
that one can “diagonalize the fusion rule” in the theoryT[M 3×S 1]. It would be interesting to understand
this from the skein module side.
57From this point of view, it might not be a coincidence that theM 3 =S 1×S 2 case—for which the
deformation cannot be applied—is also the case where we know that there are complicated torsions.
105

===== PAGE 107 =====
We hope that the present work provides a useful starting point for further investigations
along these lines.

A Some background on the construction
In this appendix, we briefly review some technical background relevant for the embedding
of sk(M3) into the gauge theory.
The topological twist
To study the Hilbert space of the twisted 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory with gauge
groupKon a spatial three-manifoldM 3, we consider the theory on the geometryM 3×R.
The topological twist is classified by homomorphisms of the Euclidean group Spin(3)E to the
R-symmetry group SU(4)R≃Spin(6) R such that at least one supercharge becomes a scalar
overM 3 under the twisted Euclidean group Spin(3)′
E.
In 4d, there are three classes of twist: the Donaldson–Witten twist (and its orientation
reversal) [93], the Vafa–Witten twist [25] (see also [117]), and the GL twist [13], which is
parametrized by a variablet∈CP 1 and, fort=±i, it is also sometimes known as the
Marcus twist [118].
On the geometryM 3×R, which has reduced holonomy, the backgrounds for the Vafa–
Witten (and its orientation reversal) and the GL/Marcus twists become identical, both
106

===== PAGE 108 =====
described by the natural embedding Spin(3) E→Spin(6) R. An equivalent way to state this
is that the spinor representation4of Spin(6) R≃SU(4) R pullbacks to2⊕2of Spin(3) E≃
SU(2)E. As a consequence, four out of the 16 supercharges now become scalars,
2×(2,4) under Spin(3) E×Spin(6)R ⇝4×1⊕4×3under Spin(3) ′
E.(A.1)
Therefore, with this partial twist alongM 3, one obtains anN= 4 supersymmetric quan-
tum mechanics (SQM) alongR, and different topological theories correspond to picking a
nilpotent linear combinations of the four supercharges.
Supercharges and R-symmetry
The SUSY algebra of the 1dN= 4 theory has an SO(4) R-symmetry, but in general it is not
a symmetry of the full theory. A genuine symmetry is the centralizer SU(2) R≃Spin(3) R⊂
Spin(6)R of the image of Spin(3)E, which is already present in the Vafa–Witten theory or as
an enhancement of the U(1) “ghost-number” symmetry in the Kapustin–Witten theory.
To describe its action on the supercharges, we first label them asQ 1,ℓ,Q 2,ℓ,Q 1,r and
Q2,r. The notation reflects the fact that two among the four preserved supercharges of the
1d theory were left-moving in 4d before the twist, while the other two were right-moving.
In the Lorentzian signature, they would satisfy reality conditionsQ †
1,ℓ =Q 1,r andQ†
2,ℓ =
Q2,r, and one can think of them as linear combinations of the four real superchargesQ α,i of
the 3dN= 2 “T[M 3] theory” obtained by reducing a 6d (2,0) theory. From this perspective,
the first indexα={1,2}denotes the two components ofQ’s as Majorana spinors in 3d,
which form a vector representation of SO(2) E of the 3d Minkowski space, whilei={1,2}
is the index for the two-dimensional vector representation of the SO(2) R symmetry. These
two abelian symmetries each pick out some special complex combinations, with
Qℓ :=Q 11 +iQ 12−iQ 21 +Q 22 andQ r :=Q 11 +iQ 12 +iQ 21−Q 22 (A.2)
being the two “eigenstates” of SO(2) R with charge 1, and
Q1 :=Q 11 +iQ 21 andQ 2 :=Q 12 +iQ 22 (A.3)
giving two weight-1 irreducible representations of SO(2) E. The latter two are in fact just
Q1,r andQ 2,r (modulo normalization factors), while the first two can also be written as
Qℓ =Q 1,ℓ +iQ 2,ℓ andQ r =Q 1,r +iQ 2,r, which explains the choice for their subscripts.
To make the two groups more symmetric, one can instead choose the latter two to be
Qr =Q 1 +iQ 2 andQℓ =Q 1−iQ 2, which are simultaneous eigenstates under both SO(2) R
and SO(2)E.
107

===== PAGE 109 =====
When reducing to 1d, SO(2) R and SO(2)E will be part of the SO(4) R-symmetry of the
N= 4 supersymmetric quantum mechanics, enhanced to the two subgroups SU(2) R and
SU(2)R′ that respectively act on (Q 1,Q 2) and (Q ℓ,Qr) as doublet. The actions of their
centers are identical, generated by−I∈SO(4) that flips all real supercharges.
Another nice property of these linear combinations is that they are automatically nilpo-
tent in the 1d theory,Q 2
ℓ =Q 2
r =Q 2
1 =Q 2
2 = 0. In fact, any complex linear combinations
uQℓ +vQ r oru′Q1 +v′Q2 are nilpotent. Conceptually, this can be understood as a conse-
quence of the absence of suitable operators with weight two under either of the two U(1)
factors in the Cartan of the SO(4) R-symmetry. More concretely, this follows from the fact
thatQ 2
α,i∝His proportional to the Hamiltonian while anticommuting with each other in
the SQM.
Although it is perfectly justified to work with the Minkowski spacetime in our setup, to
relate the supercharges of the 1dN= 4 theory with nilpotent supercharges after topological
twists on general four-manifolds (i.e. not assumed to be justM 3×R), it is convenient to work
in the Euclidean signature. There, each of the four real supercharges can be regarded as
complex, or, equivalently, the complex supercharges can be treated as independent of their
complex conjugates.
ThenQ ℓ andQ r are the two complex supercharges preserved in the Kapustin–Witten
theory, whileQ 1 andQ 2 are preserved in the Vafa–Witten theory. From the 6d perspective,
they generate respectively a (1,1) and a (0,2) supersymmetry algebra in the remaining two
spacetime dimensions after a partial twist onM 4. WhenM 4 =M 3×S 1 (i.e. with the Eu-
clidean timeRcompactified), the two twists involves the same supersymmetric background,
but the choice of the supercharges is still different, living inside respectively a (1,1) and
(0,2) subalgebra of the 2d (2,2) theoryT[M 3×S 1].
The SU(2)R symmetry preserves “chirality” (in either the 4d sense or the 2d/SO(2) E
sense, which are equivalent) and, as it is a genuine symmetry, can be used to relateQ 1
andQ 2 (or any complex linear combinations of them). The quotient of the naive SO(4) R-
symmetry by this genuine symmetry is the “wrong” SO(3)R′, which can be thought of as the
Z2-quotient of the SU(2)R′ that acts on the doublet (Qℓ,Qr) with different chiralities. From
the point of view of the twisting parametert, this SO(3) acts transitively on theCP 1≃S 2
wherettakes values via rotations and therefore relates different topological twists given by
Qt :=Q ℓ +tQ 1,r.
Since we have the linear relation
Qr =Q 1 +iQ 2,(A.4)
the Kapustin–Witten theory onM 3×Ratt=∞is equivalent to the Vafa–Witten theory.
108

===== PAGE 110 =====
Hilbert space
The 1d theory onRhas a Hilbert spaceH full, from which one can construct the Hilbert
spaceH(M 3) of the twisted theory by taking theQ t-cohomology. The spaceH(M 3) naively
depends on both the coupling constantτof the 4d theory as well as the twist parametert.
However, the twisted theory only depends on them via a combination—referred to as the
“canonical parameter” in [13]—given by
Ψ = τ+ τ
2 + τ− τ
2
t−t −1
t+t −1

.(A.5)
It is a complex variable, but the theory is invariant under Ψ7→Ψ + 1, and one can use its
exponentialq=e 2πiΨ to parametrizeH(M 3).
The action of SU(2) R onH full is expected to be a symmetry. However, as it transforms
Qt, in general it is not a symmetry of the Hilbert space of the twisted theory. Instead, it
guarantees that a family of twists given by the orbit ofQ t under the SU(2)R action all lead
to equivalent theories. For the Cartan of SU(2)R, it acts onQ t via a phase, thus becoming a
ghost-number symmetry on the space ofQ r-closed states inH full. On the other hand, while
the action of the SO(3) quotient still preserves nilpotency of the supercharge, it doesn’t
preserve the canonical parameter Ψ, which is one way to see that it is not a true symmetry
of the theory as the twisted theories can be inequivalent for different values of Ψ. 58
It is also easy to see that there cannot be more general nilpotent supercharges beyond the
SO(4)-orbits ofQ t. In fact, any complex linear combinations of the four real supercharges
can be transformed toQ 11 +c·Q 21 up to SO(4) rotations and a complex scalar. Nilpotency
then requiresc=±i, but the two choices are also related by the SU(2) R′ action. Therefore,
all nilpotent supercharges are in the SO(4) orbit ofQ 11 +iQ 21 =Q 1, which also includeQ ℓ,
Qr and their linear combinations.
In our construction of the embedding of the skein module into the dual ofH(M 3), we
can consider arbitrary values oftin the beginning. However, to use the deformation of the
Vafa–Witten theory, we have to restrict tot=∞and use the right-moving superchargeQ r.
Then we have Ψ =τ, andq=e 2πiτ takes values inside the unit circle. To access points on
the unit circle, one needs to consider the strong-coupling limit with Imτ→0. It might be
desirable to map it to a theory with weak couplings usingS-duality, which is our next topic.
58We thank Pavel Safronov for pointing out an erroneous claim regarding the R-symmetry action in the
first version of the manuscript as well as providing other helpful comments.

===== PAGE 111 =====
S-duality
Under SL(2,Z), the canonical parameter transforms as
Ψ7→ aΨ +b
cΨ +d.(A.6)
The generatorS∈SL(2,Z) sends the coupling constantτto− 1
ngτ withn g being the lacing
number ofg(in general a collection of numbers, one for each simple or abelian summand of
g). Then one can check that the canonical parameter also transforms as
S: Ψ7→− 1
ngΨ.(A.7)
TheS-duality acts on supercharges and, consequently, on thet-parameter as
t7→ |τ|
τ ·t.(A.8)
Notice that the action is by a phase and hast= 0 and∞fixed. Therefore, one can
consistently useQ r in the construction ofH(M 3) on both sides of the duality.
Many aspects of theS-duality of the 4dN= 4 super–Yang–Mills theory have been
studied in the literature, and one fact we used in the paper is that the relative theory—
the one with mutually non-local Wilson and ’t Hooft operators but being consistent as a
boundary condition of a 5d TQFT—also hasS-duality. One way to construct this theory is
by compactifying a relative 6d (2,0) theory onT 2 (possibly with an automorphism twist in
order to obtain non-simply-lace factors) and restrict oneself to the neighborhood of an SCFT
point of the moduli space. The SL(2,Z) is then interpreted as the mapping class group of
T 2 (when an automorphism twist is turned on, the true dualities live in a subgroup that
preserves the twist). The relative theory can have both electric and magnetic symmetries,
giving rise to a doubly graded Hilbert space onM 3.
Boundary conditions and line operators
In [20], a Neumann-like boundary condition is constructed to realize the analytically contin-
ued Chern–Simons theory on the boundary of the topologically twistedN= 4 theory. This
boundary condition can be lifted to 6d (or M-theory forA-type gauge groups) as the tip of
a Melvin cigar.
On the boundary, one can construct supersymmetric Wilson lines that are compatible
with the nilpotent supercharge. After the topological twist, they then become topological
line operators in the analytically continued Chern–Simons theory. One crucial property we
110

===== PAGE 112 =====
used throughout the paper is that they obey skein relations. Another way to state this is
that, for any state|ψ⟩∈H(M 3) (e.g. viewed as an “incoming” one fromx=∞), it gives
a functional on skein classes of line operators on thex= 0 boundary. Turning this around
and assuming that there are enough “test states,” one obtains an embedding of sk(M 3) into
H(M3)∨.
While the above boundary condition is realized by the D3-NS5 system (potentially at
a non-trivial theta angle), itsS-dual is realized by the D3-D5 system, where there can be
supersymmetric ’t Hooft line operators. The dual boundary condition and the ’t Hooft
operators are used in the paper to enlarge the skein module to the enriched version eS.
To be more precise, bothKand LKtheories have the two types of boundary conditions
and they are dual to each other by switching the type and changingKto its Langlands/GNO
dual,
BD5(K)7→B NS5(LK),andB NS5(K)7→B D5(LK).(A.9)
To generate eS, we need to useB D5(K) andB NS5(K) decorated with, respectively, Wilson
and ’t Hooft lines, as well as more general dyonic lines living on (p,q)-type boundary condi-
tions. Notice that, although we say thatB D5(K),B NS5(K) andB (p,q)(K) are dual boundary
conditions, they are actually on the “same side” of theS-duality.
B Finite-group gauge theory
In this appendix, we derive some results on finite-group gauge theories used in the paper.
They are sometimes referred to as Dijkgraaf–Witten theories [119], although we will not
turn on a non-trivial Dijkgraaf–Witten twist/cocycle. Many aspects of the theory has been
studied in [120] with various results scattered in the mathematical and physics literature.
We are interested in 4d theories with gauge groupsG=S 3,S 4,S 5, D8, or Q 8. In partic-
ular, we want to compute the partition function on Σ×T 2 expressed as a function of the
genusgof Σ. As this is the partition function of a semisimple 2d TQFT, it takes the general
form
Zg(G) :=Z(Σ×T 2;G) =
X
i∈Λ
λg−1
i ,(B.1)
and our task is to determine the set of labels Λ and the “fusion eigenvalues”λ i.
B.1 Compactification
One can use the two circle factors to reduce the 4d theory to three and two dimensions.
Such a finite-group gauge theory on a circle will have holonomy sectors labeled by conjugacy
111

===== PAGE 113 =====
classesC(G) ofG. In the sector given by a holonomy [x]∈C(G), the gauge group is broken
to the stabilizer subgroupG x in lower dimensions. The 3d theory in each sector counts the
flatG x-connections over Σ, leading to
Zg(G) =
X
[x]∈C(G)
|M(Σ,Gx)|,(B.2)
where we have used the notationM(Σ,G x) := Hom(π1(Σg),Gx)/Gx for the moduli space of
flatG x-connections.
The expression above is manifestly integral, but is not of the “canonical form.” To obtain
a TQFT-like formula, one can perform this operation one more time to get to 2d,
Zg(G) =
X
(x,y)∈M(T 2,G)
|Hom(π1(Σg),Gx,y)|/|Gx,y|.(B.3)
Here, the sum is over the commuting pairs (x,y) inGmodulo conjugation, andG x,y is the
centralizer of such a pair. Now, each summand is the partition function of a 2d TQFT,
|Hom(π1(Σg),Gx,y)|/|Gx,y|=
X
R∈Irr(Gx,y)
|Gx,y|
dimR
2g−2
,(B.4)
where the sum is over irreducible representations (“irreps”) ofGx,y. This procedure also gives
a way to think of Λ as the union over irreducible representations ofG x,y for all commuting
pairs, with the fusion eigenvalues given by (|G x,y|/dimR) 2. It is a well-known result in
representation theory that this ratio is an integer, which guarantees that the entire partition
function is always integral wheng≥1.
We now work out these data explicitly for the finite groups of interest.
B.2S 3
There are three types of commuting pairs, the trivial pair (e,e), these containinga= (12),
and these withb= (123). For the second type, there are three classes: (e,a), (a,e) and
(a,a), while for the third type there are four classes: (e,b), (b,e), (b,b), and (b,b 2)∼(b 2,b).
The centralizers for the three types are respectivelyS 3,Z 2 andZ 3, and except for a two-
dimensional representation ofS 3, all the other irreps are one dimensional. Therefore, we
have the following list of fusion eigenvalues:
•for the first type,{6,6,3};
•for the second class,{2,2}×3;
112

===== PAGE 114 =====
•for the third class,{3,3,3}×4.
Putting them together, we have the partition function given by
Zg(S3) = 2·6 2g−2 + 13·3 2g−2 + 6·2 2g−2.(B.5)
Wheng= 1, this gives 21, which is also the number of commuting triples modulo conjugation.
Forg= 0, we haveZ 0(S3) = 3, which is the number of irreps ofS 3. This agrees with the
expectation that the partition function of the 4d finite-group gauge theory onS 2×T 2 is the
same as the partition function of the 2d gauge theory onT 2 with the same gauge groupG,
which equals the number of irreps (or conjugacy classes).
B.3S 4
There are five conjugacy classes inS 4, represented by the identitye, transpositiona= (12),
3-cycleb= (123), 4-cyclec= (1234) and the double transpositiond= (12)(34)∼d ′ =c 2 =
(13)(24).
For commuting pairs, there turns out to be 21 conjugacy classes:
•(e,e) with the centralizerS 4 and fusion eigenvalues{24,24,12,8,8};
•(e,a), (a,e) and (a,a) with the centralizerZ 2×Z2 and fusion eigenvalues{4,4,4,4}×3;
•(e,b), (b,e), (b,b) and (b,b 2) with the centralizerZ 3 and fusion eigenvalues{3,3,3}×4;
•(e,c), (c,e), (c,c), (c,c 2), (c 2,c), and (c,c 3) with the centralizerZ 4 and fusion eigen-
values{4,4,4,4}×6;
•(e,d), (d,e) and (d,d) with the centralizer D 8 and fusion eigenvalues{8,8,8,8,4}×3;
•(a,d), (d,a), (a,ad) and (d,d ′) with the centralizerZ 2×Z 2 and fusion eigenvalues
{4,4,4,4}×4.
Collecting these, we have
Zg(S4) = 2·24 2g−2 + 122g−2 + 14·8 2g−2 + 55·4 2g−2 + 12·3 2g−2.(B.6)
The partition function onT 4 isZ 1(S4) = 84, which is used for computing the dimension
of sk(T 3,F 4), whileZ 0(S4) = 5 equals the number of irreps ofS 4, providing a consistency
check.

We end our exploration in finite gauge theory with two simpler cases, D 8 and Q8, which
are relevant for computations in theB- andD-series.
B.5D 8 andQ 8
The dihedral group D 8 is a subgroup of O(2) that acts as the symmetry of a square. It is
generated by an order-4 rotationrand a reflectionswith the relationrsr=r −1. The eight
elements of D 8 can be presented as{e,r,r 2,r 3,s,rs,r 2s,r 3s}, and there are 5 conjugacy
classes represented bye,r∼r 3,r 2,s∼r 2s, andrs∼r 3s. For commuting pairs, there turns
out to be 22 conjugacy classes, which can be grouped into the following types:
•(e,e), (e,r 2), (r2,e), and (r 2,r 2) with the centralizer the entire D8 and fusion eigenval-
ues{8,8,8,8,4}×4;
•(e,r), (r,e), (r,r), (r,r 2), (r 2,r) and (r,r 3) with the centralizerZ 4 and fusion eigen-
values{4,4,4,4}×6;
115

===== PAGE 117 =====
•(e,s), (s,e), (s,s), (s,r 2), (r 2,s), and (s,r 2s)∼(r 2s,s) with the centralizerZ 2×Z 2
and fusion eigenvalues{4,4,4,4}×6;
•(e,rs), (rs,e), (rs,rs), (r 2,rs), (rs,r 2), and (rs,r 3s) with the centralizerZ 4 and fusion
eigenvalues{4,4,4,4}×6;
The partition function on Σ×T 2 is then
Zg(D8) = 16·8 2g−2 + 76·4 2g−2.(B.12)
One hasZ 1 = 92 andZ 0 = 5, which agrees with the five conjugacy classes of D 8.
The answer for the quaternion group Q 8 turns out to be exactly the same,
Zg(D8) =Z g(Q8).(B.13)
Although we do not know a simple argument or a deeper reason for this, it is straightforward
to check this explicitly.
The group Q 8 ={±1,±I,±J,±K}also has 22 conjugacy classes of commuting pairs,
falling into two classes:
•(±1,±1) with the centralizer the entire Q 8 and fusion eigenvalues{8,8,8,8,4}×4;
•(±1,I), (I,±1), (I,±I), as well as 12 similar ones withIreplaced withJandK,
which have aZ 4 centralizer and fusion eigenvalues{4,4,4,4}×18.
These indeed give the same 2d TQFT as D 8. This equivalence should be a useful ingredient
in computations for theB- andD-series, which we just scratched the surface and will leave
a deeper analysis to the interested reader.
C FlatN 2-connections onT 3
In this appendix, we count components of flatN 2-connections, whereN 2 is the normalizer
of a maximal torusN(T) inK= SU(3) (or the “magnetic version”N mag
2 inside PSU(3)), on
M3 =T 3. We will count with refinement, which in the electric case is about understanding
the action ofH 1(M3,Z 3)≃Z 3
3 while in the magnetic case it is given by the grading of the
components by the mod-3-valued second Stiefel–Whitney class inH 2(M3,π 1(K))≃Z 3
3.
Another convenient way of organizing the components is by relating them to the 21 flat
S3-connections.
116

===== PAGE 118 =====
C.1 Structure ofN 2
LetT⊂SU(3) be a maximal torus, which for convenience we choose to be parametrized by
diagonal unitary matrices. The Weyl group isW=N(T)/T≃S 3, and the normalizer fits
into a split exact sequence
1→T→N(T)→S 3→1.(C.1)
Thus every element ofN(T) has a unique decomposition
g=t·w, t∈T, w∈S 3.(C.2)
Inside SU(3),S 3 can be represented as the standard set of permutation matrices, normalized
to have determinant one. In other words, a cyclewis mapped to (−1) |w|Pw with|w|being
the length ofw(as a word consisting of simple reflections). The conjugation action onT
given byt7→wtw −1 shuffles the diagonal entries oft, whileg=t·wis in general an off-
diagonal matrix with three non-vanishing entries. The groupN(T) is then identified with
all such matrices with unit determinant.
This choice of the embeddingS 3→N(T) also enables us to map flatS 3-connections to
N(T)-connections. However, what is slightly more useful for classification is a map in the
opposite direction.
C.2 Classification
We first slightly reorganize the 21 conjugacy classes of commuting triples inS 3 from the
previous appendix:
•1 trivial class (e,e,e),
•7 = 2 3−1 classes contained in a transposition subgroupZ 2 ={e,a},
•13 = (27−1)/2 classes contained in a 3-cycle subgroupZ 3 ={e,b,b 2}.
Similarly, a flatN(T)-connection on the torus is a commuting triple
(g1,g 2,g 3)∈N(T) 3,(C.3)
modulo simultaneous conjugation byN(T). Writingg i =t iwi as in (C.2), one can also
project (t 1w1, t2w2, t3w3) to a triple (w 1,w 2,w 3) inS 3
3. It is guaranteed to be commuting
(asTis abelian and normal), but this might appear ambiguous at the level ofN(T)-conjugacy
classes. However, the cycle-types of thew i are well defined, which we use to classifyN(T)-
triples. Furthermore, conjugation byN(T) basically allows to set one oft i to the identity
(unless allw i’s are trivial) and one non-trivialw i to eitheraorb. This leads to a three-fold
classification.
117

===== PAGE 119 =====
The trivial class.Ifw i =efor alli, then it is a flatT-connection, and they all lie in the
same connected component given by (T×T×T)/Wwith the diagonal Weyl group action.
The center action ofH 1(T 3,Z 3) is a symmetry of this identity component. Therefore, it
contributes once to thee= 0 sector.
TheZ 2 classes.Consider a triple lying in{e,a}for a fixed transpositiona∈S 3. The fixed

TheZ 3 classes.For triples with an element conjugate tobgeneratingZ 3⊂S 3, thet i’s

central choices give 3 2 = 9 distinct lifts, and no two are conjugate inN(T). The center
symmetry acts transitively on the 9 points, therefore splitting it as 1 + 8 with one in the
e= 0 sector and one in eight out of the 26 non-trivial electric sectors. Precisely which eight
sectors are picked depends on the choice of the triple inS 3 (and its stabilizer in SL(3,Z)).
Summing over the 13 conjugacy classes of triples restores the SL(3,Z) symmetry with the
split 13·(1 + 8) = 13 + 26·4, which has a four-dimensional space for eache̸= 0 sector.

agrees with flatS n+1-connections. If one extrapolates fromn= 1 and 2, naively for each
